ʓ yy c ʓ x dd m α v Ze n Δ o yy t h ff m t 6 w L h 49 12 … 76 s’estant laissé entendre comme il se vouloit départir du roi de Navarre 49 = comme · 76 = le roi de Navarre · 59 = argent

Guyenne → Henri III and Villeroy · homophonic figures with a numbered nomenclator · 1585–1586 · 14 of 16 documents deciphered into sentences and translated, two pages decoded, not read

Mayenne–Forget’s Cipher-1, 1585–86

→

The ciphered despatches of Mayenne’s campaign in Guyenne, on Pons and Taillebourg, credit, an unpaid army, Castets and the siege of Monségur. Michael Brackx re-transcribed them; here each leaf was deciphered sentence by sentence against the images, his figure labels corrected where the image disagrees, and translated. Four letters also have clear copies made at the time.

Daniel Bourdeau · posted 22 October 2026 · updated 23 October 2026

Method · Cipher-1 despatches read with the key on the images (ff. 110, 123v, 124v, 173, 196, 196v, 201); ff. 123r and 124r decoded, not read: read with known key (partial) · ff. 143r-v, 149v-150v, 154r-v (clear copies ff. 155-157) and fr. 15571 f. 177r-v (clear copy f. 176r-v): read from existing decipherment (complete)

With Michael Brackx (solution, key corrected, identification, transcription, verification) and Satoshi Tomokiyo (key or decipherment used) · Also credited · Contemporary key or decipherment (key or decipherment used)

known key
On this pageThe despatchesOne keyWhat they sayHow it was checkedWhat stays openFull textsSources

At a glanceKnown key applied · partial: 14 of 16 documents deciphered into sentences and translated (15,395 of 17,913 tokens, 86%); ff. 123r and 124r decoded, not read

The letters
Eight despatches of the Duke of Mayenne’s campaign in Guyenne, December 1585 to May 1586: Mayenne and Pierre Forget de Fresnes writing to Henri III and to the secretary of state Villeroy. Sixteen ciphered pages or page groups in all, 17,913 cipher tokens.
Where
Bibliothèque nationale de France, fr. 15572 ff. 110, 123–124, 143, 150, 154, 173, 196r–v, 201 and fr. 15571 f. 177, on Gallica. Catalogue item 12 here; Satoshi Tomokiyo knew of no contemporary decipherment for them.
The cipher
Mayenne–Forget’s Cipher-1: several figures and invented signs for each letter, and numbers for common words and names (12 il, 14 que, 49 comme, 76 le roi de Navarre). Some figures look so alike that a reader can only narrow them to two or three letters.
How it was deciphered
Tomokiyo’s table, checked here on two letters deciphered at the time, and Michael Brackx’s eleven per-leaf keys merged into one, decode every leaf. A decode with holes in most sentences is not a reading (George Lasry, 22 October 2026). So each leaf was then built into sentences against the full-resolution images: every break looked at, about 400 of Brackx’s figure labels corrected where the image disagrees, a few letter values added per leaf, and the result translated into English. Four letters also have clear copies made at the time (ff. 155–157, found by Brackx; fr. 15571 f. 176, found here), and the decode matches them.
What it says
A plan on Pons and Taillebourg dropped when Turenne took the field; Mayenne telling Villeroy that the King may end the war but not by ruining his reputation; no money at Marmande and the march on Sainte-Bazeille; the captain of Castets bought away from the King of Navarre; lenders who will lend only on Forget’s word; the siege of Monségur in May 1586, with Mayenne in a fever and a battery of fifteen cannon.
Still open
ff. 123r and 124r give only 41% and 56% of their sentences clean after three passes each: decoded, not read. ff. 177, 110, 123v, 124v and 173 build sentences but read at 94.1–94.9% of their words, under 95%. Holes remain in some lines of f. 110, fourteen code numbers have no value, and the label corrections have had one reader.
Credit
The transcriptions, the per-leaf keys and most code values are Michael Brackx’s (issues 64–83, October 2026, with Claude Code). He also found the clear copies on ff. 155–157. The key tables are Tomokiyo’s. The label corrections, the sentence-by-sentence decipherment, the translations, f. 196v and the copy on f. 176 are this project’s (22 October 2026).

01 The despatches

In 1585–86 Charles de Lorraine, Duke of Mayenne, led a royal army against the Protestants in Guyenne, with Marshal Matignon at Bordeaux and Pierre Forget de Fresnes in his camp. Their despatches to the King and to Villeroy are bound in BnF fr. 15572 and fr. 15571. Tomokiyo (cryptiana, Henry III’s Cipher with Ambassadors) sorts their ciphers into three. This entry holds the one he calls Mayenne–Forget’s Cipher-1. Cipher-2 and Matignon’s Cipher-3 are different ciphers, each with its own page.

“Clean sentences” below is the share of sentences with at most one hole in ten words (docs/_check_reading.py), before and after the leaf was worked on the images on 22 October 2026. Measured word by word on 24 October 2026, five leaves (fr. 15571 f. 177, ff. 110, 123v, 124v and 173) build coherent sentences but read at 94.1–94.9% of their words, just under the 95% a complete reading needs, so their extent is partial.

02 One key

Tomokiyo publishes partial tables for Cipher-1. The project checked and completed them in September 2026 against two letters in the same volume deciphered at the time: the postscript of f. 14v, written out in clear on f. 15r, and the long despatch of f. 18, deciphered on f. 19. The figures do not separate cleanly. Three shapes of “6” stand for i, n and s, and on the scans their correlation is above 0.9. So the key is kept as letter sets, not single letters, and a program scores the possible letters with a model of period French and keeps the likeliest.

In October 2026 Michael Brackx re-transcribed every Cipher-1 leaf from full-size line crops. He used a label set that keeps apart figures the earlier transcriptions had merged, and he fitted a key to each leaf. His eleven keys differ in a few letter sets, mainly whether θ is m or m/s and how e and ε divide. Here they were merged by union: each figure keeps every letter any leaf gives it. The merged key decodes every leaf at least as well as the leaf’s own key (9,855 against 9,609 tokens in runs of French). Agreement with the clear copies does not move by more than a point. The differences were in the reading of the hands, not in the cipher. Trimming rarely used letters from the merged key lowered the score, so it was not done.

Code values come from the crib leaves, the clear copies and context: 12 il, 13 qui, 14 que, 15 quoy, 17 car, 24 mais, 25 nous, 26 vous, 27 nostre, 33 vostre, 34 ainsi, 35 parce que, 36 toutesfois, 40 bien, 43 point, 46 rien, 47 tous/tout, 49 comme, 51 pas, 52 plustost, 53 affin, 54 aussi, 61 beaucoup, 62 peut, 63 après, 64 quelque, 73 the syllable oi on f. 18 and le Roy on f. 177, 76 and 78 le roi de Navarre, 82 le gouverneur de Coul[…], 84 le vicomte de Turenne, 88 Monsieur du Mayne, 104 mil. 44 is a stop. 59 is argent, added here: on f. 154r the cipher has “nous prester icy de l’[59] a rendre a Paris” where the clear copy f. 157r has “de l’argent”, and the word fits its four other places.

On 22 October 2026 each leaf was taken again on the full-resolution images, sentence by sentence (section 04). The merged key stays the key; what a single hand needs beyond it is kept in a small key per leaf (key_cipher1_<leaf>.json): on f. 110 a looped tilde for s and a large C for l or r; on f. 196v four figures not seen before (a small double cross for t, a barred figure for r/rr, a looped x for x, a looped y for z); on f. 177 three figures that are nulls, which the clear copy leaves out.

03 What they say

Quotations are from the decipherments in texts/reading_*.md, in the spelling the figures give. A word in brackets with a question mark is carried only in part by its figures; […] is a run of figures that does not form a word. Each leaf has an English translation beside it in the Full texts below.

Pons and Taillebourg, 31 December 1585 (fr. 15571 f. 177). Forget tells Villeroy of a plan to besiege Pons and Taillebourg together: taking Taillebourg would deprive the enemy of the toll on the Charente, oster un grand revenu qu’ils tirent du passaige de la Charante, and the country offered thirty thousand écus for the siege. Then Matignon had word from Bordeaux that the vicomte de Turenne estoit paru en campaigne avec forces et canons, and the despatch was rewritten. Forget comments: Voila comment noz opinions sont erantes. A clear copy of the whole cipher, made at the time, is on the preceding leaf, f. 176r–v; it agrees with the decipherment and settles the few words the figures carry only in part.

Villeneuve-d’Agenais, March 1586 (f. 110). Mayenne to Villeroy, wholly in cipher. The dateline, earlier transcribed “De Bellebourg”, reads on the image De Villeneufve d’Agenoys. He asks Villeroy for advice as a friend and states his case: Si le roi est ennuié de la guerre, et qu’il la veuile finir, il ce peult faire plus [?honnestement] que par la ruine de ma reputation. Left without the promised money, the army périra toute entière avant qu’il soit [?six] sepmaines, and Sa Maiesté en perdra plus que moi. Lines 1–4, 8, 10, 14–16, 24–25 and 36 keep holes.

Marmande, March 1586 (ff. 123–124). Mayenne to the King, opening in clear. On f. 123v he answers those who would have him live off the province as he once did in the Dauphiné: here the enemy are si meslés parmi ce qui vous reste de bons subiects que persone n’habite la campaigne, and contributions could be raised only by garrisons. He sends Forget, with the sieur de Gourgues, to borrow et argent et draps (f. 124v), and resolves d’aller attacquer Saincte Basille sitost qu’il m’aura envoié cinq canons et la pouldre et les balles; Turenne has come to Nérac.

ff. 123r and 124r are decoded, not read: the gist given here for them is pieced together from fragments and may be wrong in detail. On f. 123r more than a hundred thousand écus would be needed, there are not twelve thousand in the treasurer’s hands, and Matignon has borrowed twenty thousand; on f. 124r Mayenne proposes putting the army into garrison, or taking it into the Rouergue or the Quercy.

Castets, April 1586 (f. 143). The clear half of f. 143r reports on Marmande, Sainte-Bazeille and the capitulation of Castets. The cipher half explains a private bargain with the captain of Castets. The clear copy on f. 155r opens:

s’estant laissé entendre comme il se vouloit départir du Roy de Navarre, duquel je sçay qu’il est tres mal contant

Mayenne judged him the best soldier on Navarre’s side and treated with him: twelve thousand écus over three months, leave to sell his property, a pardon to be registered by the Parlement, and a promise not to bear arms for his party for six months. The last ten lines of the cipher, after the foot of f. 155r, were deciphered on the image: C’est se qui m’a meu d’entrer plus volontiers en ce traicté. The copy goes on at the head of f. 155v and agrees with them word for word.

Forget to Villeroy (ff. 149v–150, 154). Forget, writing in cipher under Mayenne’s clear letters, reviews the Castets bargain (“if there was a fault in the substance, there was more in the form”). He complains that the two commanders listen too readily to their own people. After Easter he reports the pay crisis: two musters owed, the captains’ complaints, and lenders who had promised money to be repaid in Paris backing away. Villeroy’s advice to raise money on exchange is answered on f. 154v. Clear copies of all three exist (ff. 155v–157r).

Credit (f. 173). To the King, 1586, from the man Mayenne sent back to the town a second time to raise money. The town will not contribute, trusting the King’s letters that it is held to nothing for this army, and il ne s’est peu trouver un seul escu soubs [?les] obligations de Monsieur du Mayne. Only some merchants of Paris and elsewhere, with money to recover in Paris, would lend sur ma foy. The page ends in mid-sentence.

Before Monségur, May 1586 (ff. 196, 201, 196v). One despatch survives twice, ff. 196r and 201r, fair copies of one enciphered draft, with different homophones and more of it in clear on f. 201. Mayenne has worked so hard at the siege qu’il y a gaigné un reume qui luy a causé la fièvre; Marshal Matignon is pressed to come; Sainct-Chamarand is ill, la Barge and Montberault carry the siege, la Binault is dead. f. 196v, which was not among the transcriptions and was transcribed here, carries the despatch on: a battery of fifteen cannon to open the day after tomorrow, and the defenders resolved to hold pour ne recevoir la honte que le roi de Navarre a faict recevoir à celui qui commandoit dans Saincte Bazeille. Navarre is leaving for Saintonge to meet his reiters, Turenne holds the province from Nérac, and the army’s wants sont quasi arrivées à l’extremité. It is dated in clear Du camp devant Monségur ce […] jour de May 1586.

04 How it was checked

  • Sentences, not tokens. George Lasry reviewed this page on 22 October 2026: the decryptions of the leaves without a copy could not be built into coherent French sentences, and he was right (5–24% of their sentences were clean). Each leaf was then taken sentence by sentence: where a sentence would not form, the figures were cropped from the full-resolution image (openapi.bnf.fr) and looked at, the label or the key value corrected, the leaf decoded again and the sentence rebuilt. The per-leaf result is in the table above. A leaf counts only when its sentences hold (0.66–0.79 clean, with the holes marked) and it has an English translation; ff. 123r and 124r do not reach that and are not counted.
  • Label corrections. About 400 of Brackx’s figure labels were changed where the image disagrees, each listed with its line, the old and new label and the word it gives (brackx2026/corrections/*.tsv): f. 110 131 in three passes, the second against a set of exemplars cut from this hand; ff. 123r–v 138; ff. 124r–v 75; ff. 196/201 46; f. 173 14; f. 177 12; f. 143v 10. Two untranscribed lines were added (f. 123r l. 37, f. 143v l. 35). The letter values a hand needs beyond the merged key are kept per leaf (key_cipher1_<leaf>.json).
  • The copies. Where a copy of the time exists, the key’s decode agrees with it on 85–93% of letters: f. 143r 0.93, f. 150r 0.85, f. 150v 0.90, f. 154r 0.89, f. 154v 0.89 and 0.93. With shuffled keys it is 0.25–0.38. Two more copies were found on 22 October 2026 and transcribed. fr. 15571 f. 176r–v is the clear copy of f. 177: compared word by word with the decipherment, it confirms 73 le Roy, 64 quelque and the null figures, and the cipher has one word (dicte) that the copy leaves out. The f. 143 copy does not stop at the foot of f. 155r: it runs on over seven lines of f. 155v, which agree with the decipherment of the last ten lines of f. 143v (the cipher has one word more, si).
  • The twin. ff. 196 and 201 carry the same figures at most positions: they are two fair copies of one enciphered draft. They were aligned figure by figure and deciphered together, and f. 201’s clear foot lines up with the first seven lines of f. 196v and gives 63 après.
  • Shuffled keys. With the key’s letter sets permuted among its figures (set sizes kept, three seeds), the decode falls to 12.1% of tokens in runs of French, against 63.7% with the key. That token figure counted short corpus words strung together (“la que le estoit”) and is kept as evidence that the key is right, not as a measure of the decipherment.
  • Leaves with no outside text (23 October 2026). ff. 173, 196v and 124v have no clear copy, no decipherment of the time and no print: each rests on the key, a figure transcription corrected by one reader, a few letter values for that leaf alone, and a reading chosen from the figures’ letter sets. Re-scored on the running text alone (an earlier sweep had counted line markers as holes), they hold 0.72, 0.885 and 0.667 of sentences with no more than one hole in ten words. Because the key gives many figures two to four letters, the readings were also aligned with their figures: they are carried on 96–97% of letters, against 45–49% for the same figures read as shuffled or unrelated French, and they use a second or third value of a set about as often as f. 143v does where the clear copy decides. They stay deciphered, with a lower confidence grade (B) than the leaves a copy checks.
  • One reader. The label corrections and the sentence-by-sentence decipherments were made by one reader (Claude) on the images. No second reader has checked them yet.

05 What stays open

  • ff. 123r and 124r, decoded, not read: 0.41 and 0.56 clean sentences after three passes each. The third pass re-cut the labels figure by figure and built an atlas of the hand’s 5,111 figures with classifiers across the leaves, and tried code values from the other leaves. The remaining breaks fall on legible figures of their ordinary shapes: they are the writer’s omissions or slips, and codes with no value. They need a clear copy or another letter in the same hand.
  • Holes inside f. 110 (ll. 1–4, the corner of l. 1 being damaged, 8, 10, 14–16, 24–25, 36), where no single key change fixes the letters.
  • Code numbers 16, 19, 23, 45, 47, 60, 65, 66, 67, 99, 101, 102, 103, 9 and the name code 98 have no value; each occurs one to three times and no copy covers them. 9 matches “Comme il” in one copy and “Mais s’il” in the other, so it is not one word. 103 falls where the f. 19 decipherment itself has an unreadable word.
  • f. 20r, the end of the f. 18 despatch, now has its clear copy of the time: the end of f. 19v, posted as written by Michael Brackx (issue #87, 23 October 2026). It is taken as the reading of f. 20r, in part (86% of its words read as sense, the expansions of abbreviations counted as holes): two of the four regiment names are unread, and the day (vj, the 6th) is doubtful. The copy has q[ue] ce fust honte at l. 5, which confirms code 14 = que; there is no wording difference between copy and cipher. It tells of the remains of four regiments and Colonel Heid’s Swiss meeting again on the 6th of next month, and of letters of Turenne to the King of Navarre, intercepted, showing that Tulle was about to compound for four or five thousand écus.
  • A second reader for the label corrections.

Full texts

The transcription and the decryption of every document on this page, so that the reading can be used and checked. Short files are shown here; all are in the repository.

f. 143r

Mayenne to the King, April 1586, after the capitulation of Castets: its captain, unhappy with the King of Navarre, has agreed to leave him; a treaty of twelve thousand ecus in three months, leave to sell his property and a pardon to be registered by the Parlement. The clear copy is f. 155r.

Decryption

f143r.txt

# fr. 15572 f. 143r, Mayenne to the King, April 1586: decryption with the merged Cipher-1 key (key_cipher1.json)
# Transcription: Michael Brackx (issues #73, #77-#79, #83), brackx2026/. Decoded here with this
# project's beam and period-French 6-gram (final_texts.py). Per manuscript line: the figures, then the
# decode cut into words. UPPER CASE = inside a run of French (measure.py sense rule); |lower case| =
# outside one (doubtful letters, unknown spellings, slips); [nn] = code group not identified.
# Measured: 529 of 704 tokens read as sense (75.1%); scrambled-key floor 11.7%.

# The contemporary clear copy (brackx2026/copies/f155r_f143_corr.txt), as read by Michael Brackx:

s'estant laissé entendre comme il se vouloit départir du Roy de Navarre, duquel je sçay qu'il est tres mal contant; et ayant considéré que je l'ay tousjours ouy tenir pour le meilleur homme de commandement qu'il ayt,
et que ce ne seroit pas petite faveur pour ses affaires que de le retenir, j'ay traicté avec luy, et en
considération d'une promesse qu'il m'a faicte de ne porter les armes pour son party de six mois, et de quelque
particulier service qu'il m'a promis de vous faire pendant ce temps; et aussi ou à la vérité il s'est résolu
quasi à tout ce que nous avons voulu pour la composition de sa place, laquelle estoit encores assez bonne pour
nous retenir à ce siège s'ils eussent voulu s'opiniastrer encores quelque temps, qui eust importé beaucoup de
despense, et encores plus de séjour, qui nous est très cher, parce qu'il m'eust fallu passer la rivière pour
y aller et puis la repasser pour venir icy. Toutes ces considérations, et pour les premières plus que pour
les autres, je luy ay verbalement promis de luy faire payer dans trois mois la somme de douze mil escus, de luy
faire obtenir permission [interlined: *de V. Mté*] de pouvoir jouir de son bien, ou avoir permission de le
vendre, oultre d'intercéder envers Vostre Majesté pour luy faire accorder un pardon de toutes les faultes
passées. Ce que je supplie très humblement Vostre Majesté de confirmer, et croire comme je n'aye rien de cela resolu que soubz vostre bon plaisir. Toutesfois que je ne me fusse pas tant advancé en ce traicté si je
n'eusse sceu que c'est assez porter à approcher(?) de beaucoup de fruict, et péril de peu de perte(?). Car la
condition de la permission de vendre son bien n'est comme rien, parce qu'il ne s'en peut ayder, estant quasi
tout sondict bien, mesme ledict lieu de Castets, du costé de sa femme et substitué à ses enfans. Et quant au
pardon, il ne peut estre vallable qu'il n'aye passé au Parlement, où il est bien certain qu'ils ne le
vérifieront pas, attendu(?) tant de charges et submissions qu'il ne s'en pourra ayder qu'il n'aye obéy aux
édicts et du tout renoncé à l'aultre party. Et aussi qu'il n'y avoit plus de hasard que pour la somme, dont je
m'estois(?) bien garanty(?) en partie par le temps qu'il nous a espargné du siège de Castets; et l'autre(?), elle
n'est pas aussi du tout inutile, ayant de luy ceste obligation de ne porter les armes de six mois pour son
party, où il eust …

# ff. 143r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, April 1586: the reading

The cipher half of the Castets despatch, read from its contemporary clear copy on f. 155r (fr. 15572, canvas 162),
as transcribed by Michael Brackx (`brackx2026/copies/f155r_f143_corr.txt`). The decode of the cipher with the
merged key (`f143r.txt`, `f143v.txt`) agrees with this copy on 93% of letters (f. 143r). `(?)` marks a word of the
copy Brackx read with doubt; `…` the point where the copy breaks off. English: `reading_f143_en.md`.

## f. 143r

s'estant laissé entendre comme il se vouloit départir du Roy de Navarre, duquel je sçay qu'il est très mal contant;
et ayant considéré que je l'ay tousjours ouy tenir pour le meilleur homme de commandement qu'il ayt, et que ce ne
seroit pas petite faveur pour ses affaires que de le retenir, j'ay traicté avec luy, et en considération d'une
promesse qu'il m'a faicte de ne porter les armes pour son party de six mois, et de quelque particulier service qu'il
m'a promis de vous faire pendant ce temps; et aussi qu'à la vérité il s'est résolu quasi à tout ce que nous avons
voulu pour la composition de sa place, laquelle estoit encores assez bonne pour nous retenir à ce siège s'ils
eussent voulu s'opiniastrer encores quelque temps, qui eust importé beaucoup de despense, et encores plus de séjour,
qui nous est très cher, parce qu'il m'eust fallu passer la rivière pour y aller et puis la repasser pour venir icy.
Toutes ces considérations, et pour les premières plus que pour les autres, je luy ay verbalement promis

## f. 143v

de luy faire payer dans trois mois la somme de douze mil escus, de luy faire obtenir permission de V. M.té de
pouvoir jouir de son bien, ou avoir permission de le vendre, oultre d'intercéder envers Vostre Majesté pour luy
faire accorder un pardon de toutes les faultes passées. Ce que je supplie très humblement Vostre Majesté de
confirmer, et croire comme je n'aye rien de cela résolu que soubz vostre bon plaisir. Toutesfois je ne me fusse
pas tant advancé en ce traicté si je n'eusse sceu que c'est assez pour approcher(?) de beaucoup de fruict, et péril
de peu de perte(?). Car la condition de la permission de vendre son bien n'est comme rien, parce qu'il ne s'en peut
ayder, estant quasi tout sondict bien, mesme ledict lieu de Castets, du costé de sa femme et substitué à ses
enfans. Et quant au pardon, il ne peut estre vallable qu'il n'aye passé au Parlement, où il est bien certain qu'ils
ne le vérifieront pas, attendu(?) tant de charges et submissions, qu'il ne s'en pourra ayder qu'il n'aye obéy aux
édicts et du tout renoncé à l'aultre party. Et aussi qu'il n'y avoit plus de hasard que pour la somme, dont je
m'estois(?) bien garanty(?) en partie par le temps qu'il nous a espargné du siège de Castets; et l'autre(?), elle
n'est pas aussi du tout inutile, ayant de luy ceste obligation de ne porter les armes de six mois pour son party,
où il eust …

# ff. 143r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, April 1586: English translation

Translated from the reading (`reading_f143.md`), which follows the contemporary clear copy on f. 155r. `(?)` marks
a word of the copy read with doubt; `…` where the copy breaks off.

## f. 143r

[The captain of Castets] having let it be understood that he wished to leave the King of Navarre, with whom I know
he is very discontented; and having considered that I have always heard him held to be the best man of command he
has, and that it would be no small favour to [Navarre's] affairs to keep him, I have treated with him. [I did so]
in consideration of a promise he has made me not to bear arms for his party for six months, and of some particular
service he has promised to do you in that time; and also because in truth he agreed to almost everything we wanted
for the surrender of his place, which was still strong enough to hold us at this siege had they chosen to hold out
obstinately a while longer. That would have cost a great deal of expense, and still more of time, which is very
dear to us, because I would have had to cross the river to get there and then cross back to come here. For all
these considerations, and for the first more than the others, I have verbally promised him

## f. 143v

to have him paid the sum of twelve thousand écus within three months; to obtain for him Your Majesty's permission
to enjoy his property, or permission to sell it; and moreover to intercede with Your Majesty to have him granted a
pardon for all his past offences. I most humbly beg Your Majesty to confirm this, and to believe that I have
settled none of it except subject to your good pleasure. Yet I would not have gone so far in this treaty had I not
known that it was enough to come near(?) a great gain, with the risk of little loss(?). For the condition of leave
to sell his property is as nothing, since he can make no use of it: almost all his said property, even the said
place of Castets, comes from his wife's side and is entailed on his children. As for the pardon, it cannot be valid
until it has passed the Parlement, where it is quite certain they will not register it, given(?) so many charges
and conditions, so that he can make no use of it until he has obeyed the edicts and wholly renounced the other
party. And there was no other risk than the sum, against which I had(?) partly covered myself(?) by the time he
saved us in the siege of Castets; and the other(?) is not wholly useless either, since we have from him this
undertaking not to bear arms for his party for six months, where he would have …

[Continued in `reading_f143v_tail_en.md`, from the cipher on the image and the rest of the copy on f. 155v.]

33  6 Ze t Hc Ze o 7 v ao ei x v Ze n t 47 h y dd x c Ze t n ao q 7 x t w 7 v Ze
    N TENTION AIENT LE TOUTES CESTE LA UO |ee dont|
34  3 h Tm t z g o q x v y ao B 8 t w h Tm D c ao L 7 he t L x Tm B q z 7 g X q n
    |ie mes uiuan| SIEGE DE MA PAROLE REMIS SOU AU |l|

# ff. 143r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, April 1586: the reading

The cipher half of the Castets despatch, read from its contemporary clear copy on f. 155r (fr. 15572, canvas 162),
as transcribed by Michael Brackx (`brackx2026/copies/f155r_f143_corr.txt`). The decode of the cipher with the
merged key (`f143r.txt`, `f143v.txt`) agrees with this copy on 93% of letters (f. 143r). `(?)` marks a word of the
copy Brackx read with doubt; `…` the point where the copy breaks off. English: `reading_f143_en.md`.

## f. 143r

s'estant laissé entendre comme il se vouloit départir du Roy de Navarre, duquel je sçay qu'il est très mal contant;
et ayant considéré que je l'ay tousjours ouy tenir pour le meilleur homme de commandement qu'il ayt, et que ce ne
seroit pas petite faveur pour ses affaires que de le retenir, j'ay traicté avec luy, et en considération d'une
promesse qu'il m'a faicte de ne porter les armes pour son party de six mois, et de quelque particulier service qu'il
m'a promis de vous faire pendant ce temps; et aussi qu'à la vérité il s'est résolu quasi à tout ce que nous avons
voulu pour la composition de sa place, laquelle estoit encores assez bonne pour nous retenir à ce siège s'ils
eussent voulu s'opiniastrer encores quelque temps, qui eust importé beaucoup de despense, et encores plus de séjour,
qui nous est très cher, parce qu'il m'eust fallu passer la rivière pour y aller et puis la repasser pour venir icy.
Toutes ces considérations, et pour les premières plus que pour les autres, je luy ay verbalement promis

## f. 143v

de luy faire payer dans trois mois la somme de douze mil escus, de luy faire obtenir permission de V. M.té de
pouvoir jouir de son bien, ou avoir permission de le vendre, oultre d'intercéder envers Vostre Majesté pour luy
faire accorder un pardon de toutes les faultes passées. Ce que je supplie très humblement Vostre Majesté de
confirmer, et croire comme je n'aye rien de cela résolu que soubz vostre bon plaisir. Toutesfois je ne me fusse
pas tant advancé en ce traicté si je n'eusse sceu que c'est assez pour approcher(?) de beaucoup de fruict, et péril
de peu de perte(?). Car la condition de la permission de vendre son bien n'est comme rien, parce qu'il ne s'en peut
ayder, estant quasi tout sondict bien, mesme ledict lieu de Castets, du costé de sa femme et substitué à ses
enfans. Et quant au pardon, il ne peut estre vallable qu'il n'aye passé au Parlement, où il est bien certain qu'ils
ne le vérifieront pas, attendu(?) tant de charges et submissions, qu'il ne s'en pourra ayder qu'il n'aye obéy aux
édicts et du tout renoncé à l'aultre party. Et aussi qu'il n'y avoit plus de hasard que pour la somme, dont je
m'estois(?) bien garanty(?) en partie par le temps qu'il nous a espargné du siège de Castets; et l'autre(?), elle
n'est pas aussi du tout inutile, ayant de luy ceste obligation de ne porter les armes de six mois pour son party,
où il eust …

[The copy continues on f. 155v (canvas 163 left), ll. 1-7, transcribed 22 Oct 2026 in `brackx2026/copies/f155v_f143_tail.txt`; the rest of f. 143v, read from the cipher on the image and checked against it, is in `reading_f143v_tail.md`.]
Reading

reading_f143v_tail.md

# f. 143v, ll. 26–35, Mayenne to Henri III, April 1586: the reading after the copy's break

fr. 15572 f. 143v, Gallica canvas 151, left page. The contemporary clear copy on f. 155r (`reading_f143.md`) ends at
the foot of that page on *où il eust …*. Those words fall in manuscript l. 26 of the cipher, at the figures
`… 12 x g z Ze` (*il eust*). This file reads the cipher from there to its end (l. 26, from code 62, to l. 35).

What was done, 22 Oct 2026: the figures (Brackx's transcription) were read against the letter sets of the merged
key, and each line was cropped from the full-resolution image (openapi.bnf.fr IIIF) and checked. The copy does not
stop at the break. It runs on over the page to the head of **f. 155v (canvas 163, left page)**, seven lines and a
docket. This continuation had not been transcribed and is now in `brackx2026/copies/f155v_f143_tail.txt`. The cipher
reading below was made from the figures. The copy was then used as a check, and the figures carry every word of it.
The cipher has one word more (*si*, l. 27). Ten label changes, including one untranscribed line (l. 35), are in
`brackx2026/corrections/f143v.tsv`. The corrected figures are in `brackx2026/corrections/f143v_cipher.txt`.

Marks: `[?word]` = carried only in part by its figures; `[…]` = figures that do not form a word. Accents and
punctuation are modern. The spelling is what the figures give. English: `reading_f143v_tail_en.md`.

## The cipher, from the copy's break

… ayant de luy ceste obligation de ne porter les armes de six mois pour son party, où il eust [l. 26] peu nuire pour
beaucoup [?davantaige], [l. 27] et si j'ay grande espérance de l'en retirer du [l. 28] tout. Et ce faisant, de
pouvoir [?tirer] de bons services, [l. 29] et par son moyen et exemple retirer d'aultres [l. 30] hommes et places de
conséquence.

C'est se qui m'a [l. 31] meu d'entrer plus volontiers en ce traicté.

[l. 32] Sur quoy Vostre Majesté me fera, s'il luy plaist, entendre son [l. 33] intention, ayant le tout excepté la
somme dont [l. 34] je me suis engaigé de ma parole, remis soubz [l. 35] le bon plaisir de […].

## Notes on the words

- *peu* is code 62 (*peut/peu*). *beaucoup* is code 61, which the file had as a bare 6 (see corrections).
- *davantaige* ("more"): w ao g X v Ze o B y + = d-a-u-a-n-t-a-i-g-?. The last figure is a plain cross. Elsewhere on this
  leaf a plain cross stands for r (*tire[r]*, l. 28), and the key gives it m/s/i. The copy has *davantage*. All
  letters but the last are carried, hence the `[?]`.
- *et si j'ay*: x m q o ei ao B = e-t-s-i-j-a-y. The copy has *Et j'ay*: the copyist dropped *si*.
- *[?tirer]*: Ze o L h + = t-i-r-e-? with the same plain cross as l. 26 (copy *tirer*).
- *services*: y q h L g o | dd t dd = s-?-e-r-v-i-c-e-s. The q is one figure too many (q = s/o/u): a slip of the
  encipherer, or a figure that has been misread. The o was missing from the transcription.
- *hommes*, *somme*: the x with a bar over it is one figure for *mm* (f 7 x̄ t dd = h-o-mm-e-s;
  q 7 x̄ t = s-o-mm-e). Brackx labels it plain x.
- *C'est se qui*: f x z m | z x 13. The z figure (ꝺ) is s, as in *conséquence* on the line above, so the
  figures spell *se* for *ce* (copy *ce*).
- *Sur quoy Vostre Majesté*: z g L 15 68. These are codes 15 = *quoy* and 68 = *Vostre Majesté*; the file had
  them as x and 6 8.
- *soubz le bon*: z 7 g λ q | n t λ 7 v. Brackx labels the λ figure lam (= b) in *de bons* l. 28 but X (= a) here.
  l. 35 was not in the transcription.
- *de […]*: the last four figures of the cipher, `fig o gg T`. The first is a long stroke looped at both ends and
  crossed by a bar, a figure not seen elsewhere on these leaves. Where they stand, the copy has *Vostre Ma.té*, but the
  figures do not spell it, and Vostre Majesté is code 68 two lines above. They are left open. After them, on the same
  line, the hand writes *me.* in ordinary letters and then goes on in clear.

## Remaining holes

Four: the closing figures (l. 35), and three single letters carried by a figure whose value does not fit (the plain
cross in *davantaig[e]* and *tire[r]*, the extra q in *s[q]ervices*). Every other word is carried by its figures,
and the copy on f. 155v agrees with all of them.

## The clear text that follows (ll. 35–39, outside the cipher)

Read from the image only and not checked against another witness; `(?)` = doubtful.

[l. 35] *me.* Hier(?) Mons.r le Mar.al faict [l. 36] sortir ceux qui estoient dedans lad. place de Castets, et
faict desja(?) […] commancer [l. 37] à la razer(?), à quoy ceux du païs contribuent si volontiers que ce sera bien
tost faict. [l. 38] J'attendz icy dans deux jours led. s.r Mar.al avec toutte sa(?) trouppe(?). Et ay cependant
[l. 39] commancé ce jourd'huy de faire investir ceste place, dont les ennemis sont fort b[…] …

The page ends there. The f. 155v copy does not carry these lines: after the cipher's last words it gives only the
docket, *M.r du Mayne du x(?)me avril 1586*, so the despatch is from April 1586. The day is not certain: it may be *xme* (10th).
Translation

reading_f143_en.md

# ff. 143r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, April 1586: English translation

Translated from the reading (`reading_f143.md`), which follows the contemporary clear copy on f. 155r. `(?)` marks
a word of the copy read with doubt; `…` where the copy breaks off.

## f. 143r

[The captain of Castets] having let it be understood that he wished to leave the King of Navarre, with whom I know
he is very discontented; and having considered that I have always heard him held to be the best man of command he
has, and that it would be no small favour to [Navarre's] affairs to keep him, I have treated with him. [I did so]
in consideration of a promise he has made me not to bear arms for his party for six months, and of some particular
service he has promised to do you in that time; and also because in truth he agreed to almost everything we wanted
for the surrender of his place, which was still strong enough to hold us at this siege had they chosen to hold out
obstinately a while longer. That would have cost a great deal of expense, and still more of time, which is very
dear to us, because I would have had to cross the river to get there and then cross back to come here. For all
these considerations, and for the first more than the others, I have verbally promised him

## f. 143v

to have him paid the sum of twelve thousand écus within three months; to obtain for him Your Majesty's permission
to enjoy his property, or permission to sell it; and moreover to intercede with Your Majesty to have him granted a
pardon for all his past offences. I most humbly beg Your Majesty to confirm this, and to believe that I have
settled none of it except subject to your good pleasure. Yet I would not have gone so far in this treaty had I not
known that it was enough to come near(?) a great gain, with the risk of little loss(?). For the condition of leave
to sell his property is as nothing, since he can make no use of it: almost all his said property, even the said
place of Castets, comes from his wife's side and is entailed on his children. As for the pardon, it cannot be valid
until it has passed the Parlement, where it is quite certain they will not register it, given(?) so many charges
and conditions, so that he can make no use of it until he has obeyed the edicts and wholly renounced the other
party. And there was no other risk than the sum, against which I had(?) partly covered myself(?) by the time he
saved us in the siege of Castets; and the other(?) is not wholly useless either, since we have from him this
undertaking not to bear arms for his party for six months, where he would have …

[Continued in `reading_f143v_tail_en.md`, from the cipher on the image and the rest of the copy on f. 155v.]
Translation

reading_f143v_tail_en.md

# f. 143v, ll. 26–35, Mayenne to Henri III, April 1586: English

Translation of `reading_f143v_tail.md`, with the same holes. It begins inside the sentence where the f. 155r copy
breaks off.

## The cipher, from the copy's break

… since we have from him this undertaking not to bear arms for his party for six months, where he [l. 26] could have
done [?much more] harm; [l. 27] and I have great hope of drawing him away from it [l. 28]
altogether. In doing so, I hope to be able to [?get] good service [l. 29] from him, and through his means and
example to draw away other [l. 30] men and places of consequence.

That is what [l. 31] moved me to enter into this treaty more willingly.

[l. 32] On which Your Majesty will, if it please you, let me know your [l. 33] intention. I have left the whole
of it, except the sum to which [l. 34] I have pledged my word, subject [l. 35] to the good pleasure of […].

(*Pour beaucoup davantaige*: "by a good deal more"; the f. 155v copy has *pour beaucoup davantage*. The closing figures stand where the copy has *Vostre Majesté*, "Your Majesty", but
they do not spell it.)

## The clear text that follows (outside the cipher)

Yesterday(?) the Marshal had those who were inside the said place of Castets come out, and has already(?) […]
begun to raze(?) it, to which the people of the country contribute so willingly that it will soon be done. I expect
the said Marshal here in two days with all his(?) troops(?). Meanwhile I have begun today to have this place
invested, of which the enemy are very b[…] …

Docket of the copy (f. 155v): *M.r du Mayne*, April 1586; the day may be the 10th.

f. 149v-150r

Forget to Villeroy, April 1586, on how the composition of Castets was made: 'if there was a fault in the substance, there was more in the form'. The clear copy is f. 155v.

# The contemporary clear copy (brackx2026/copies/f155v_f150r.txt), as read by Michael Brackx:

Comme il estoit bien besoing, estimant(?) qu'il avoit résolu la composition de Castets d'un façon qui n'eust
pas à mon advis esté trouvée bonne, car dans la mesme capitulation de Castets, qui y avoit compris ceux qui
sont du party contraire, et faisant leur accordant douze mil escus pour les démolitions de sa maison, qui
estoit comme une amende honorable, et qu'ils pourroient sans changer de religion paisiblement jouir de leurs
biens, qui estoit du tout rompre l'édict, et puis de leur accorder une abolition générale. Il a esté résolu
ainsi que vous aurez veu par la despesche. Et la capitulation de la place qui a esté rendue(?) est signée(?),
n'est que la forme qu'elle debvoit estre, et ce n'est que une promesse verbale, qui est contre tout son plus
honneste prétexte. Mais s'il y avoit eu de la faulte en l'effect, il y en avoit eu plus en la forme, car ceste
capitulation avoit esté tant précipitée pendant mon absence qu'elle estoit comme résolue sans l'advis et
consultation de Mr le Mareschal, qui en estoit avec raison très irrité. Et estant bien vray(?) moins(?) Sr du
Mayne s'estant sur ce si mal séparé d'ensemble que ledict sieur Mareschal estoit résolu de s'en retourner(?) et
s'en alloit à Bordeaux. Toutesfois je suis tant allé et tant venu(?) entre l'un et l'autre que les
capitulations ayant esté reformées, comme je vous dis, ce qui estoit de leur part a esté fort bien retenu(?), et
vous peux asseurer qu'ils sont maintenant très bien ensemble, et si les … qu'ils ne se séparoient pour une
compagnie ny d'amitié(?) ; car je vous proteste que je n'y sçay aucune rais

# ff. 149v–150v, Forget to Villeroy, April 1586: the reading

Read from the contemporary clear copy on ff. 155v–156r (fr. 15572, canvases 162–163), as transcribed by Michael
Brackx (`brackx2026/copies/f155v_f150r.txt`, `f156_f150v_corr.txt`). The decode of the cipher with the merged key
(`f150r.txt`, `f150.txt`) agrees with the copy on 85% (f. 150r) and 90% (f. 150v) of letters. Light normalisation of
word division and punctuation; `(?)` marks a word Brackx read with doubt, `[…]` a word he could not read. English:
`reading_f150_en.md`.

## ff. 149v–150r

Comme il estoit bien besoing, estimant(?) qu'il avoit résolu la composition de Castets d'une façon qui n'eust pas à
mon advis esté trouvée bonne: car dans la mesme capitulation de Castets il y avoit compris ceux qui sont du party
contraire, et faisant leur accordant douze mil escus pour les démolitions de sa maison, qui estoit comme une amende
honorable, et qu'ils pourroient sans changer de religion paisiblement jouir de leurs biens, qui estoit du tout rompre
l'édict, et puis de leur accorder une abolition générale. Il a esté résolu ainsi que vous aurez veu par la despesche.
Et la capitulation de la place qui a esté rendue(?) et signée(?) n'est que la forme qu'elle debvoit estre, et ce
n'est qu'une promesse verbale, qui est contre tout son plus honneste prétexte. Mais s'il y avoit eu de la faulte en
l'effect, il y en avoit eu plus en la forme, car ceste capitulation avoit esté tant précipitée pendant mon absence
qu'elle estoit comme résolue sans l'advis et consultation de M.r le Mareschal, qui en estoit avec raison très irrité.
Et estant bien vray(?) moins(?) [que] le S.r du Mayne s'estant sur ce si mal séparé d'ensemble, ledict sieur
Mareschal estoit résolu de s'en retourner(?) et s'en alloit à Bordeaux. Toutesfois je suis tant allé et tant venu(?)
entre l'un et l'autre que, les capitulations ayant esté reformées comme je vous dis, ce qui estoit de leur part a
esté fort bien retenu(?), et vous peux asseurer qu'ils sont maintenant très bien ensemble, et si les […] qu'ils ne se
séparoient pour une compagnie ny d'amitié(?); car je vous proteste que je n'y sçay aucune raison

## f. 150v

pourquoy ils le puissent faire, si ce n'est que l'un et l'autre escoutent trop volontiers leurs gens, qui ne
vouldroient les uns point de compagnie [des] autres.

Pour le supérieur, je vous en ay voulu escrire l'histoire au vray, affin que s'il s'en parle par delà, comme il se
pourra faire, vous soiez bien informé de la vérité du faict, qui est, Dieu mercy, passé assez doucement, encores qu'il
y eust grande apparence du contraire. Je n'avois point pensé d'avoir encore de ruine(?) en ceste armée, mais en cecy
j'estime aucunement avoir gaigné mes gages(?), comme peut estre ils le vous confesseront bien tous deux l'un et
l'autre.

# ff. 149v–150v, Forget to Villeroy, April 1586: English translation

Translated from the reading (`reading_f150.md`), which follows the contemporary clear copy on ff. 155v–156r.
`(?)` marks a word read with doubt, `[…]` an unread word.

## ff. 149v–150r

As was very needful, since(?) he had settled the surrender of Castets in a way that would not, in my opinion, have
been found good. For in the capitulation of Castets itself he had included those of the opposing party, granting
them twelve thousand écus for the demolition of [the captain's] house, which was like a public penance; that they
might peacefully enjoy their property without changing religion, which was to break the edict outright; and then
granting them a general amnesty. It was settled as you will have seen from the despatch. And the capitulation of the
place, as surrendered(?) and signed(?), is only in the form it ought to have been, and is no more than a verbal
promise, which goes against his most honest pretext. But if there was a fault in the substance, there was more in
the form: this capitulation had been so rushed during my absence that it was as good as settled without the advice
and consultation of the Marshal, who was rightly very angry. And it being true(?) that the Sieur du Mayne [Mayenne]
had parted from him so badly over it, the said Marshal had resolved to go back(?) and was leaving for Bordeaux.
Nevertheless I went back and forth(?) between the one and the other so much that, the capitulations having been
reworked as I tell you, what concerned them was very well kept(?), and I can assure you they are now on very good
terms; and if the […] that they did not part over a company nor in friendship(?); for I assure you I know no reason

## f. 150v

why they should do so, unless it is that both listen too readily to their own people, who on each side want
nothing to do with the other.

As for the above, I wanted to write you the true story of it, so that if it is spoken of over there, as may happen,
you are well informed of the truth of the matter, which, thank God, passed off quietly enough, although there was
every sign of the contrary. I had not expected to see any more ruin(?) in this army; but in this I think I have
somewhat earned my wages(?), as perhaps both of them will admit to you.

f. 150v

The same letter continued: the two commanders listen too readily to their own people, who want no company of one another; Forget thinks he has earned his wages. The clear copy is f. 156r.

## ff. 149v–150r

why they should do so, unless it is that both listen too readily to their own people, who on each side want

f. 154r

Forget to Villeroy after Easter 1586: the pay of two musters owed, captains' complaints, and lenders who had promised money to be repaid in Paris drawing back; forty thousand ecus. The clear copy is f. 157r.

# The contemporary clear copy (brackx2026/copies/f157_f154r_corr.txt), as read by Michael Brackx:

En quelle peine nous estions lors pour le payement des deux monstres qui avoient esté faictes à noz gens de

vouloit faire banqueroute, il est cuidé advenir une sédition à l'espagnolle parmy eulx, avec des langages et
des juremens(?) si estranges qu'il n'est possible de plus, jusques à vouloir abandonner les gardes et menasser de plier les
enseignes. Monsieur du Mayne est en une telle peine que je vous asseure qu'il en est pitoyable, estant
en perpétuelle appréhension de veoir advenir quelque grand scandale en ceste armée, et n'ayant moyen d'y
pourvoir si promptement que l'occasion [le requiert](?), et parce que ceux qui nous avoient promis de nous prester icy de l'argent à rendre à Paris, lors
que nous y avons envoyé le commis du Trésorier avec noz obligations et promesses, ils ont faict de nouvelles
difficultez et protesté qu'ils ne presteroient pas un escu que je ne m'obligeasse seul en mon privé nom, et que
je ne leur fisse promettre de bouche que les deniers leur seroient rendus à Paris a leur veue, de
sorte qu'il est force que je sois icy retenu pour ceste occasion, dont je vois bien que je ne sortiray que je
ne sois obligé pour plus de quarante mil escus en mon privé nom, qui sera remarqué pour une pure folie, qui
considérera le temps où nous sommes, qu'il peut

# f. 154r–v, Forget to Villeroy, after Easter 1586: the reading

Read from the contemporary clear copy on f. 157r (fr. 15572, canvas 164), as transcribed by Michael Brackx
(`brackx2026/copies/f157_f154r_corr.txt`, `f157_f154v.txt`). The decode of the cipher with the merged key
(`f154.txt`, `f154v.txt`) agrees with the copy on 89% (f. 154r) and 89–93% (f. 154v) of letters; code 59 = *argent*
was fixed from this copy. Light normalisation of punctuation; `(?)` marks a word Brackx read with doubt. English:
`reading_f154_en.md`.

## f. 154r

En quelle peine nous estions lors pour le payement des deux monstres qui avoient esté faictes à noz gens de guerre,
laquelle est devenue encores depuis beaucoup plus grande. Parce que lesdits payements n'ayant peu suivre lesdites
monstres si tost que de coustume, aucuns capitaines ayans imprimé aux soldats que l'on les vouloit faire banqueroute,
il est cuidé advenir une sédition à l'espagnolle parmy eulx, avec des langages et des juremens(?) si estranges qu'il
n'est possible de plus, jusques à vouloir abandonner les gardes et menasser de plier les enseignes. Monsieur du Mayne
est en une telle peine que je vous asseure qu'il en est pitoyable, estant en perpétuelle appréhension de veoir
advenir quelque grand scandale en ceste armée, et n'ayant moyen d'y pourvoir si promptement que l'occasion le
requiert(?). Et parce que ceux qui nous avoient promis de nous prester icy de l'argent à rendre à Paris, lorsque nous
y avons envoyé le commis du Trésorier avec noz obligations et promesses, ils ont faict de nouvelles difficultez et
protesté qu'ils ne presteroient pas un escu que je ne m'obligeasse seul en mon privé nom, et que je ne leur fisse
promettre de bouche que les deniers leur seroient rendus à Paris à leur veue; de sorte qu'il est force que je sois
icy retenu pour ceste occasion, dont je vois bien que je ne sortiray que je ne sois obligé pour plus de quarante mil
escus en mon privé nom, qui sera remarqué pour une pure folie, qui considérera le temps où nous sommes, qu'il peut

## f. 154v

advenir tel inconvénient qu'il me ruineroit de fonds en comble. Mais l'appréhension d'un aussi plus grand malheur me
le faict moins considérable. J'ay esté contrainct de faire des lettres de change de toutes ces sommes sur M.r
Castille. Je vous supplie, Monsieur, faire donner ordre qu'elles soient acquittées, vous pouvant asseurer que je ne
l'eusse pas faict si volontiers sans ce qui est dans l'une de voz despesches, où vous nous mandez que nous debvons
chercher de prendre de l'argent à change le plus que nous pourrons.

Monsieur, ce n'est plus pour faire aucunes prédictions, mais je ne vous sçaurois celer que s'il n'est autrement
pourveu à ceste armée, dans la fin du mois prochain il ne se trouvera pas deux mil hommes ensemble; car ils publient
tout haut que l'armée de Daulphiné est payée tous les mois et que celle-cy est l'armée desfavorisée, avec des propos
si fascheux que je ne puis les vous répéter. Et vous puis dire que ceulx qui sont tenus les plus sages le sont en
cela quasi moins que les autres.

# f. 154r–v, Forget to Villeroy, after Easter 1586: English translation

Translated from the reading (`reading_f154.md`), which follows the contemporary clear copy on f. 157r. `(?)` marks a
word read with doubt.

## f. 154r

[You saw] what difficulty we were then in over the payment of the two musters that had been held for our soldiers,
and it has since grown much greater. Because the payments could not follow the musters as quickly as usual, some
captains put it into the soldiers' heads that they were to be cheated, and a mutiny in the Spanish manner nearly
broke out among them, with language and oaths(?) as outrageous as can be, to the point of wanting to abandon their
guard posts and threatening to furl the colours. Monsieur du Mayne [Mayenne] is in such distress that I assure you
it is pitiful, in constant fear of seeing some great disorder break out in this army, and with no means of providing
against it as quickly as the occasion requires(?). And since those who had promised to lend us money here, to be
repaid in Paris, when we sent the Treasurer's clerk to them with our bonds and promises, have raised new difficulties
and declared that they would not lend a single écu unless I bound myself alone in my own name, and unless I had it
promised to them by word of mouth that the money would be repaid to them in Paris on sight, I am forced to stay here
for this reason; and I see well that I shall not get away without being bound for more than forty thousand écus in
my own name. That will be taken for pure folly by anyone who considers the times we live in, in which there may

## f. 154v

befall such a mishap as would ruin me from top to bottom. But the fear of a still greater misfortune makes it count
for less with me. I have been obliged to draw bills of exchange for all these sums on Monsieur Castille. I beg you,
Monsieur, to give orders that they be paid, and I can assure you I would not have done it so readily but for what is
in one of your despatches, where you tell us we should try to raise as much money on exchange as we can.

Monsieur, it is no longer a matter of making predictions, but I cannot hide from you that unless this army is
otherwise provided for, by the end of next month there will not be two thousand men left together. For they say
openly that the army of Dauphiné is paid every month and that this one is the out-of-favour army, with talk so ugly
that I cannot repeat it to you. And I can tell you that those held to be the wisest are, in this, almost less so
than the others.

f. 154v

The same letter continued in two cipher blocks: Villeroy's advice to raise money on exchange, and the difficulty of doing it at Bordeaux. Clear copy f. 157r.

f. 173 read in part

To the King, 1586: the credit crisis; no means to pay two musters, the town refuses to contribute on the strength of the King's letters, no one will stand surety, and only merchants of Paris lend on the writer's word.

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (95% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

# decode cut into words. UPPER CASE = inside a run of French (measure.py sense rule); |lower case| =
# outside one (doubtful letters, unknown spellings, slips); [nn] = code group not identified.

Reading

reading_f173.md

# f. 173, [Forget] to Henri III, 1586: the reading

fr. 15572 f. 173, Gallica canvas 180, right page. The letter opens in clear and goes on in Cipher-1 for
thirty-three lines, on Mayenne's want of money and the failure of credit in the town. The writer is the man Mayenne
sent back to the town for the second time. No clear copy of this leaf is known.

What was done, 22 Oct 2026: the transcription by Michael Brackx was decoded with the merged key and then read
sentence by sentence. Every break was cropped from the full-resolution image and checked there. Fourteen labels were
corrected and three letter values added for this leaf; the corrections, the corrected figures, the leaf key and the
passes are listed in the files named at the end.

Marks: a word in square brackets with a question mark is carried only in part by its figures; the ellipsis in
brackets is a run of figures that does not form a word; 44 is the stop code. Accents and punctuation are modern;
the spelling is what the figures give.

## The clear opening

Sire, Vostre Majesté a esté suffisamment advertie par les deux dernières depesches de Monsieur du Mayne

## The cipher

[l. 1] […] [44] de l'extrême nécessité en [?laquelle] [l. 2] il se [?retrouve], se voyant frustré de la venue
[l. 3] de troisiesme mois, qu'il y a plus de six sepmaines [l. 4] qu'il estimoit estre par les chemins.

Et n'ayant [l. 5] aucun moyen de faire payer les gens de [?guerre] [l. 6] des deux monstres qu'il a esté contraint
de [?leur] [l. 7] faire faire, dont il est importuné avec [l. 8] […] rigueurs et irreverences [?inaccoustumées],
[l. 9] à quoy il n'a trouvé autre remède que de me renvoyer […] [l. 10] pour la seconde fois, tant pour prier ceux
[l. 11] du [?corps] de ceste ville de […] vouloir [?secourir] [l. 12] en cest affaire, que pour essayer de recouvrer
[l. 13] de l'argent soubs ses obligations, à quelque interest [l. 14] que ce peust estre.

Mais ces deux moyens sont [l. 15] aussi peu servis […] de l'un que [?l']autre.

Car de ceux [l. 16] de la ville, qui [?n'ont] faict que [?opposer] leur [?nécessité] [l. 17] à la nostre, et se
[?fortiffier] en leur excuse [l. 18] des lettres qu'ils dient avoir de Vostre Majesté, par [l. 19] lesquelles elle
leur promet qu'ils ne seront [l. 20] tenus à aucune contribution pour ceste [l. 21] [?armée]. S'estant asseurez
[?sur] lesdictes [l. 22] lettres que Vostre Majesté y a entièrement pourveu, [l. 23] […] l'exemple du général, les
[?particuliers] [l. 24] ne se sont pas trouvés moins refroidis.

Car [l. 25] il ne s'est peu trouver un seul escu soubs [?les] [l. 26] obligations de Monsieur du Mayne, ny aucun de
ses serviteurs [l. 27] et amys qu'il a par deçà, qui ne se sont voulu [l. 28] aucunement obliger ny respondre pour
[l. 29] luy.

Et s'il ne se fust trouvé quelques [?marchans] [l. 30] de Paris et de dehors qui avoient de l'argent [l. 31] à
recouvrer audict Paris, qui ont voulu [?prendre] [l. 32] ceste confiance sur ma foy que de se [l. 33] contanter de
le me bailler sur ma simple …

[The page ends here; the sentence runs on beyond f. 173r.]

Files: `brackx2026/corrections/f173.tsv` (label changes), `brackx2026/corrections/f173_cipher.txt` (corrected
figures), `key_cipher1_f173.json` (leaf key), `reading_f173_log.md` (passes), `reading_f173_en.md` (English).

reading_f173_en.md

# f. 173, [Forget] to Henri III, 1586: English translation

Translated from the reading (`reading_f173.md`), with the same holes. A word in square brackets with a question mark
translates a word the figures carry only in part; `[…]` is a run of figures that does not form a word; `[44]` the
stop code. Line numbers are those of the cipher.

## The clear opening

Sire, Your Majesty has been sufficiently informed by the last two despatches of Monsieur du Mayne

## The cipher

[l. 1] […] [44] of the extreme necessity in [?which] [l. 2] he [?finds] himself, seeing himself cheated of the
arrival [l. 3] of the third month, which for more than six weeks [l. 4] he thought to be on the road.

And having [l. 5] no means of paying the men of [?war] [l. 6] for the two musters he has been forced to [?have them]
[l. 7] make, for which he is pressed with [l. 8] […] harshness and disrespect [?unheard of], [l. 9] for which he has
found no other remedy than to send me back […] [l. 10] for the second time, both to beg those [l. 11] of the
[?body] of this town to […] be willing to [?help] [l. 12] in this business, and to try to raise [l. 13] money on his
bonds, at whatever interest [l. 14] it might be.

But these two means have [l. 15] served as little […] the one as the [?other].

For of those [l. 16] of the town, who [?have] done nothing but [?set] their [?need] [l. 17] against ours, and
[?entrench] themselves in their excuse [l. 18] of the letters they say they have from Your Majesty, by [l. 19] which
she promises them that they shall not be [l. 20] held to any contribution for this [l. 21] [?army]. Being assured
[?by] the said [l. 22] letters that Your Majesty has provided for it entirely, [l. 23] […] the example of the whole
body, the [?private persons] [l. 24] have not been found any less cooled.

For [l. 25] not a single écu could be found on [?the] [l. 26] bonds of Monsieur du Mayne, nor [on those of] any of
his servants [l. 27] and friends he has here, who would not by any means [l. 28] bind themselves or answer for
[l. 29] him.

And had there not been some [?merchants] [l. 30] of Paris and from outside, who had money [l. 31] to recover in the
said Paris, who were willing to [?take] [l. 32] this trust on my word as to [l. 33] be content to hand it to me on my
mere …

[The page ends here; the sentence runs on beyond f. 173r.]

# ff. 196 and 201, Mayenne's camp to Henri III, before Monségur, May 1586: the reading

One despatch enciphered twice in the Cipher-1 key, fr. 15572 f. 196r (canvas 203 right) and f. 201r (canvas 208
right); f. 201 has clear text before, inside and after its cipher. The despatch goes on over f. 196v, a page of
cipher not transcribed, signed in clear *Du camp devant Monségur ce … de May 1586*.

Done 22 Oct 2026: Brackx's two transcriptions aligned figure by figure and decoded jointly with the merged key, every
break checked on the Gallica images; 46 labels changed, 3 image defects noted (`brackx2026/corrections/f196_201.tsv`).
Passes, failures and open points: `reading_f196_201_log.md`. English: `reading_f196_201_en.md`.

Markers `[l. 12]` are f. 196's lines, `[f. 201 l. 28]` f. 201's. Spelling as ciphered, lightly regularised.
`[?word]` partly supported, `[…]` not read, `[37]` code not identified; braces give f. 201's wording where it
differs; clear text in italics.

## The fever

[l. 1] *Sire, depuis le partement du sieur de Bosseval, Monsieur du Mayne vous a faict une autre despesche du
vingt-neufiesme du moys passé pour vous rendre raison du commencement de ce siège, auquel* il a depuis qu'il y est
[l. 2] arrivé tant travaillé qu'il y a gaigné un reume [l. 3] qui luy a causé la fièvre, laquelle, comme je
[l. 4] croy, se reglera en tierce, en ayant eu [l. 5] aujourd'huy le second accès, qui n'a, Dieu mercy,
[l. 6] aucune violance ny mauvais accident; et [l. 7] espère que sitost il en sera guary.

## Matignon called

N'ayant [37] [l. 8] cependant voulu faillir de [?vous] en advertir {d'en advertir} [l. 9] Vostre Majesté, de peur
que cest advis vous venant de quelque [l. 10] autre part, le mal ne vous fust [?récité] plus [l. 11] grand que, Dieu
mercy, il n'est.

J'en ay dès hier [l. 12] adverty Monsieur le Mareschal de Matignon, [l. 13] comme je fais encores [?…tement], pour
le [l. 14] supplier de venir icy, où je m'asseure qu'il ne [l. 15] fauldra pas de se rendre incontinant, nous ayant
[l. 16] escript auparavant qu'il eust ceste nouvelle qu'il [l. 17] partiroit dans deux jours; lesquels je suis seur
{certain} [l. 18] qu'il advancera encores quand il aura [l. 19] receu ma première, [?…] estant tousjours si […],
[l. 20] mais maintenant plus que jamais très [l. 21] requise.

## The siege

Nous avons aussi Monsieur de [l. 22] Sainct-Chamarand malade, de sorte que quasi tout le faix de [l. 23] ce siège
est demouré à messieurs de la [l. 24] Barge et {et de} Montberault, qui y travaillent aussi [l. 25] extrêmement.

Nous avons perdu de maladie le sieur de la [?Binault], [l. 26] qui avoit servy de [l. 27] mareschal de camp.

Toutesfois {[x 6]} [?l'armée], qui estoit près de [l. 28] mondict sieur le Mareschal, qui les y eust ja
[l. 29] soulagés. Pour le regard dudict siège, il [l. 30] s'advance fort {in clear: Le regard dudict siège il
s'advance fort}.

Toutesfois, parce que la ville est grande et [l. 31] malaisée à assiéger, il a fallu faire des {f. 196r ends}
[f. 201 l. 28] tranchées en plusieurs [f. 201 l. 29] endroicts; et estans mal garnis de pionniers,
[f. 201 l. 30] la besongne en a esté [?plus longue].

*[Je] croy qu'après demain l'on commencera la batterye de quinze canons, dont il y a apparence qu'ils
s'estonneront, n'ayans pas accoustumé à bien assiéger les places et faire avec cest équipaige. Toutesfoys jusques
icy ils sont toutte à Monsieur …* (f. 201, clear foot)
Translation

reading_f196_201_en.md

# ff. 196 and 201, Mayenne's camp to Henri III, before Monségur, May 1586: English translation

Translation of `reading_f196_201.md`, with the same holes. `[?word]` = doubtful, `[…]` = not read, `[37]` = code
group not identified; braces give f. 201's wording where it differs; italics = clear text on the leaves. Line markers
as in the French.

## The fever

[l. 1] *Sire, since the departure of the sieur de Bosseval, Monsieur du Mayne has sent you another despatch, of the
29th of last month, to give you an account of the beginning of this siege, at which* he has, since he [l. 2] arrived
there, worked so hard that he has caught a cold [l. 3] which has given him a fever; which, I [l. 4] believe, will
settle into a tertian, he having had [l. 5] today the second fit, which has had, God be thanked, [l. 6] no violence
nor any bad turn; and [l. 7] I hope that he will soon be cured of it.

## Matignon called

Having [37] [l. 8] not wished, however, to fail to inform [?you] of it {to fail to inform} [l. 9] Your Majesty, for
fear that, this news reaching you from some [l. 10] other quarter, the illness might be [?reported] to you as [l. 11]
greater than, God be thanked, it is.

Since yesterday I have [l. 12] informed the Marshal de Matignon, [l. 13] as I am doing again [?…ly], to [l. 14] beg
him to come here, where I am sure he will not [l. 15] fail to come at once, he having [l. 16] written to us, before
he had this news, that he [l. 17] would set out within two days; which I am sure {certain} [l. 18] he will bring
forward when he has [l. 19] received my first letter, [?…] being always so […], [l. 20] but now more than ever very
[l. 21] necessary.

## The siege

We also have Monsieur de [l. 22] Saint-Chamarand ill, so that almost the whole burden of [l. 23] this siege has
fallen on Messieurs de La [l. 24] Barge and {and de} Montberault, who are also working at it [l. 25] extremely hard.

We have lost to illness the sieur de La [?Binault], [l. 26] who had served as [l. 27] marshal of the camp.

However {[x 6]} [?the army], which was with [l. 28] the said lord Marshal, would already have [l. 29] relieved them
there. As for the said siege, it [l. 30] is advancing well {in clear: As for the said siege, it is advancing well}.

However, because the town is large and [l. 31] hard to besiege, it has been necessary to dig {f. 196r ends}
[f. 201 l. 28] trenches in several [f. 201 l. 29] places; and being short of pioneers, [f. 201 l. 30] the work has
taken [?longer].

*I believe that the day after tomorrow the battery of fifteen cannon will open, at which there is every sign that
they will take fright, not being used to seeing places properly besieged and handled with such an equipment.
Nevertheless, so far they are all for Monsieur …* (f. 201, clear foot)

f. 201

The same despatch as f. 196, enciphered again with different homophones and with more of it in clear; the two were aligned figure by figure.

# ff. 196 and 201, Mayenne's camp to Henri III, before Monségur, May 1586: the reading

One despatch enciphered twice in the Cipher-1 key, fr. 15572 f. 196r (canvas 203 right) and f. 201r (canvas 208
right); f. 201 has clear text before, inside and after its cipher. The despatch goes on over f. 196v, a page of
cipher not transcribed, signed in clear *Du camp devant Monségur ce … de May 1586*.

Done 22 Oct 2026: Brackx's two transcriptions aligned figure by figure and decoded jointly with the merged key, every
break checked on the Gallica images; 46 labels changed, 3 image defects noted (`brackx2026/corrections/f196_201.tsv`).
Passes, failures and open points: `reading_f196_201_log.md`. English: `reading_f196_201_en.md`.

Markers `[l. 12]` are f. 196's lines, `[f. 201 l. 28]` f. 201's. Spelling as ciphered, lightly regularised.
`[?word]` partly supported, `[…]` not read, `[37]` code not identified; braces give f. 201's wording where it
differs; clear text in italics.

## The fever

[l. 1] *Sire, depuis le partement du sieur de Bosseval, Monsieur du Mayne vous a faict une autre despesche du
vingt-neufiesme du moys passé pour vous rendre raison du commencement de ce siège, auquel* il a depuis qu'il y est
[l. 2] arrivé tant travaillé qu'il y a gaigné un reume [l. 3] qui luy a causé la fièvre, laquelle, comme je
[l. 4] croy, se reglera en tierce, en ayant eu [l. 5] aujourd'huy le second accès, qui n'a, Dieu mercy,
[l. 6] aucune violance ny mauvais accident; et [l. 7] espère que sitost il en sera guary.

## Matignon called

N'ayant [37] [l. 8] cependant voulu faillir de [?vous] en advertir {d'en advertir} [l. 9] Vostre Majesté, de peur
que cest advis vous venant de quelque [l. 10] autre part, le mal ne vous fust [?récité] plus [l. 11] grand que, Dieu
mercy, il n'est.

J'en ay dès hier [l. 12] adverty Monsieur le Mareschal de Matignon, [l. 13] comme je fais encores [?…tement], pour
le [l. 14] supplier de venir icy, où je m'asseure qu'il ne [l. 15] fauldra pas de se rendre incontinant, nous ayant
[l. 16] escript auparavant qu'il eust ceste nouvelle qu'il [l. 17] partiroit dans deux jours; lesquels je suis seur
{certain} [l. 18] qu'il advancera encores quand il aura [l. 19] receu ma première, [?…] estant tousjours si […],
[l. 20] mais maintenant plus que jamais très [l. 21] requise.

## The siege

Nous avons aussi Monsieur de [l. 22] Sainct-Chamarand malade, de sorte que quasi tout le faix de [l. 23] ce siège
est demouré à messieurs de la [l. 24] Barge et {et de} Montberault, qui y travaillent aussi [l. 25] extrêmement.

Nous avons perdu de maladie le sieur de la [?Binault], [l. 26] qui avoit servy de [l. 27] mareschal de camp.

Toutesfois {[x 6]} [?l'armée], qui estoit près de [l. 28] mondict sieur le Mareschal, qui les y eust ja
[l. 29] soulagés. Pour le regard dudict siège, il [l. 30] s'advance fort {in clear: Le regard dudict siège il
s'advance fort}.

Toutesfois, parce que la ville est grande et [l. 31] malaisée à assiéger, il a fallu faire des {f. 196r ends}
[f. 201 l. 28] tranchées en plusieurs [f. 201 l. 29] endroicts; et estans mal garnis de pionniers,
[f. 201 l. 30] la besongne en a esté [?plus longue].

*[Je] croy qu'après demain l'on commencera la batterye de quinze canons, dont il y a apparence qu'ils
s'estonneront, n'ayans pas accoustumé à bien assiéger les places et faire avec cest équipaige. Toutesfoys jusques
icy ils sont toutte à Monsieur …* (f. 201, clear foot)

reading_f196_201_en.md

# ff. 196 and 201, Mayenne's camp to Henri III, before Monségur, May 1586: English translation

Translation of `reading_f196_201.md`, with the same holes. `[?word]` = doubtful, `[…]` = not read, `[37]` = code
group not identified; braces give f. 201's wording where it differs; italics = clear text on the leaves. Line markers
as in the French.

## The fever

[l. 1] *Sire, since the departure of the sieur de Bosseval, Monsieur du Mayne has sent you another despatch, of the
29th of last month, to give you an account of the beginning of this siege, at which* he has, since he [l. 2] arrived
there, worked so hard that he has caught a cold [l. 3] which has given him a fever; which, I [l. 4] believe, will
settle into a tertian, he having had [l. 5] today the second fit, which has had, God be thanked, [l. 6] no violence
nor any bad turn; and [l. 7] I hope that he will soon be cured of it.

## Matignon called

Having [37] [l. 8] not wished, however, to fail to inform [?you] of it {to fail to inform} [l. 9] Your Majesty, for
fear that, this news reaching you from some [l. 10] other quarter, the illness might be [?reported] to you as [l. 11]
greater than, God be thanked, it is.

Since yesterday I have [l. 12] informed the Marshal de Matignon, [l. 13] as I am doing again [?…ly], to [l. 14] beg
him to come here, where I am sure he will not [l. 15] fail to come at once, he having [l. 16] written to us, before
he had this news, that he [l. 17] would set out within two days; which I am sure {certain} [l. 18] he will bring
forward when he has [l. 19] received my first letter, [?…] being always so […], [l. 20] but now more than ever very
[l. 21] necessary.

## The siege

We also have Monsieur de [l. 22] Saint-Chamarand ill, so that almost the whole burden of [l. 23] this siege has
fallen on Messieurs de La [l. 24] Barge and {and de} Montberault, who are also working at it [l. 25] extremely hard.

We have lost to illness the sieur de La [?Binault], [l. 26] who had served as [l. 27] marshal of the camp.

However {[x 6]} [?the army], which was with [l. 28] the said lord Marshal, would already have [l. 29] relieved them
there. As for the said siege, it [l. 30] is advancing well {in clear: As for the said siege, it is advancing well}.

However, because the town is large and [l. 31] hard to besiege, it has been necessary to dig {f. 196r ends}
[f. 201 l. 28] trenches in several [f. 201 l. 29] places; and being short of pioneers, [f. 201 l. 30] the work has
taken [?longer].

*I believe that the day after tomorrow the battery of fifteen cannon will open, at which there is every sign that
they will take fright, not being used to seeing places properly besieged and handled with such an equipment.
Nevertheless, so far they are all for Monsieur …* (f. 201, clear foot)

f. 196v

The continuation of the ff. 196/201 despatch, dated in clear 'Du camp devant Monségur ce [...] jour de May 1586': the siege of Monségur, a battery of fifteen cannon to open the day after tomorrow, the defenders resolved to hold out after Navarre's treatment of the commander of Sainte-Bazeille, Navarre leaving for Saintonge to meet his reiters while Turenne holds the province from Nérac, the army short of money and the third month's pay.

# f. 196v, fr. 15572 (Gallica canvas 204 left): the continuation of the despatch of ff. 196r/201, 36 cipher lines,
# transcribed 22 Oct 2026 figure by figure from openapi.bnf.fr IIIF full-resolution crops (deskewed 0.68 deg), in
# Brackx's label set as corrected for this hand in corrections/f196_201.tsv (same scribe as f. 196r).
# One manuscript line per line. Not transcribed by Brackx; no second reader has checked these labels.
# Labels as in key_cipher1_f196_201.json, plus (new on this page; values from the running text, see
# texts/reading_f196v_log.md):
#   tt  small double cross with a curl below (not Brackx's large plain #, Hc): t/tt  (abattre, batterie, mettre,
#       attendons, l'attente)
#   fZ  the Ze figure with an f-ascender and a cross-bar: r/rr  (arrivees, pourvoira, irreparables)
#   xl  x with a loop (Brackx #77's "x"): x  (Xaintonge)
#   yz  a gamma-like y with a looped tail, distinct from the g-like y: z  (quinze, Bazeille)
#   dl  used for the short hooked long-s without descender (Tomokiyo's long s = c): c
#   ca  c with a stroke (corrections/f196_201.tsv): a ; ps  p with a curl: s ; pb  p-shaped b: b ; lam  lambda: b
#   codes 63 (apres, from f. 201's clear "qu'apres demain"), 65 and 99 (not identified)
# ~x = a figure struck through by the writer (l. 17), not decoded.

# f. 196v, Mayenne's camp to Henri III, before Monségur, May 1586: the reading

The verso of fr. 15572 f. 196 (Gallica canvas 204 left): 36 lines of Cipher-1 that continue the despatch of
ff. 196r/201 (`reading_f196_201.md`) from *il a fallu faire des*, then the clear close and dateline. Never
transcribed before.

Done 22 Oct 2026: the page transcribed figure by figure from full-resolution openapi.bnf.fr crops in Brackx's label
set as corrected for this scribe (`brackx2026/corrections/f196v_cipher.txt`, 1,236 figures), decoded with
`key_cipher1_f196_201.json` plus five values recovered here, and read sentence by sentence against each figure's
letter set; every break was re-cropped and looked at. f. 201's clear tail served as the crib for ll. 1–7. Passes and
failures: `reading_f196v_log.md`. English: `reading_f196v_en.md`.

Markers `[l. 12]` are the manuscript lines; a marker stands before the word in which the line begins. Spelling as ciphered, lightly regularised; accents and apostrophes added.
`[?word]` partly supported by its figures, `[…]` not read, `[65]` code not identified; clear text in italics.

## The siege

[f. 196r ends: *Toutesfois, parce que la ville est grande et malaisée à assiéger, il a fallu faire des*]
[l. 1] tranch[?e]s en plusieurs endroicts. Et estans [l. 2] mal garnis de pioniers, la besongne en a [l. 3] esté
plus longue.

Je croi que après demain l'on [l. 4] commencera à battre en batterie de quinze canons, dont [l. 5] il y a apparence
qu'ils s'estonneront, n'ayans [l. 6] pas acoustumé de veoir assiéger les places en [l. 7] ce pais avec cest
equipaige.

Toutesfois jusques icy ils [l. 8] font toute demonstrance de se vouloir [l. 9] deffendre, pour ne recevoir la honte
que le roi de Navarre a faict [l. 10] recevoir à celui qui commandoit dans Saincte [l. 11] Bazeille, lequel il est
certain qu'il a faict [l. 12] mettre prisonier.

Et si il se parle qu'il luy veult [l. 13] faire pas […].

## Navarre and Turenne

Nous avons advis qu'il doibt partir [l. 14] dans peu d'iours de [99] pour s'en aler en [l. 15] Xainton[?g]e, et font bruit
entre eux que c'est pour [l. 16] recueilir […] de ses amis, et de là aler au devant [l. 17] de ses reistres.

Le sieur viconte de Turenne est [l. 18] tousiours à Nerac, d'où il ne se parle point qu'il doive partir;
et est [l. 19] l'opinion commune que la charge de toute [l. 20] ceste province luy doibt demourer [l. 21] pendant
l'absence dudict sieur roi de Navarre.

## The army's needs

Vostre Majesté a esté advertie [l. 22] par les precedentes depesches, et encores plus [l. 23] [?p]articulierement
par le sieur de [?Senegal], [l. 24] des necessitez de ceste armée, tant d'argent que de [65], [l. 25] lesquelles
empirent tant tous les iours que les ne [l. 26] se sent plus guères augmenter, car elles sont quasi
[l. 27] arrivées à l'extremité.

À quoy Vostre Majesté pourvoira, s'il [l. 28] luy plaist, parce que ce [?n'a] peu[t] en un instant prouvoir
[l. 29] des mauls, qui sont [?après] irreparables.

Nous attendons [l. 30] par la premiere depesche certitude du [l. 31] partement du troisies[?m]e mois, dont l'attente
vous est [l. 32] bien chère, aussi que Vostre Majesté aura sceu par la derniere [l. 33] depesche.

Et comme il est aussi bien necessaire de faire [l. 34] entendre promptement vostre volonté sur la proposition
[l. 35] qui vous sera faict[?e] par [?Seval], pour ce qui se doibt [l. 36] entreprendre au partir de ce si[?e]ge.

## The clear close

*Sire, je supplye très humblement le Createur vous donner en parfaicte santé très longue et très heureuse vye. Du
camp devant Monségur ce […] jour de May, 1586*.

*Vostre très humble, très obeissant et très fidelle —*

(The signature is not on the page.)
