[f. 196r ends: *Toutesfois, parce que la ville est grande et malaisée à assiéger, il a fallu faire des*]
[l. 1] tranch[?e]s en plusieurs endroicts. Et estans [l. 2] mal garnis de pioniers, la besongne en a [l. 3] esté
plus longue.

Je croi que après demain l'on [l. 4] commencera à battre en batterie de quinze canons, dont [l. 5] il y a apparence
qu'ils s'estonneront, n'ayans [l. 6] pas acoustumé de veoir assiéger les places en [l. 7] ce pais avec cest
equipaige.

Toutesfois jusques icy ils [l. 8] font toute demonstrance de se vouloir [l. 9] deffendre, pour ne recevoir la honte
que le roi de Navarre a faict [l. 10] recevoir à celui qui commandoit dans Saincte [l. 11] Bazeille, lequel il est
certain qu'il a faict [l. 12] mettre prisonier.

Et si il se parle qu'il luy veult [l. 13] faire pas […].

## Navarre and Turenne

Nous avons advis qu'il doibt partir [l. 14] dans peu d'iours de [99] pour s'en aler en [l. 15] Xainton[?g]e, et font bruit
entre eux que c'est pour [l. 16] recueilir […] de ses amis, et de là aler au devant [l. 17] de ses reistres.

Le sieur viconte de Turenne est [l. 18] tousiours à Nerac, d'où il ne se parle point qu'il doive partir;
et est [l. 19] l'opinion commune que la charge de toute [l. 20] ceste province luy doibt demourer [l. 21] pendant
l'absence dudict sieur roi de Navarre.

## The army's needs

Vostre Majesté a esté advertie [l. 22] par les precedentes depesches, et encores plus [l. 23] [?p]articulierement
par le sieur de [?Senegal], [l. 24] des necessitez de ceste armée, tant d'argent que de [65], [l. 25] lesquelles
empirent tant tous les iours que les ne [l. 26] se sent plus guères augmenter, car elles sont quasi
[l. 27] arrivées à l'extremité.

À quoy Vostre Majesté pourvoira, s'il [l. 28] luy plaist, parce que ce [?n'a] peu[t] en un instant prouvoir
[l. 29] des mauls, qui sont [?après] irreparables.

Nous attendons [l. 30] par la premiere depesche certitude du [l. 31] partement du troisies[?m]e mois, dont l'attente
vous est [l. 32] bien chère, aussi que Vostre Majesté aura sceu par la derniere [l. 33] depesche.

Et comme il est aussi bien necessaire de faire [l. 34] entendre promptement vostre volonté sur la proposition
[l. 35] qui vous sera faict[?e] par [?Seval], pour ce qui se doibt [l. 36] entreprendre au partir de ce si[?e]ge.

## The clear close

*Sire, je supplye très humblement le Createur vous donner en parfaicte santé très longue et très heureuse vye. Du
camp devant Monségur ce […] jour de May, 1586*.

*Vostre très humble, très obeissant et très fidelle —*

(The signature is not on the page.)
Translation

reading_f196v_en.md

# f. 196v, Mayenne's camp to Henri III, before Monségur, May 1586: English

Translation of `reading_f196v.md`, with the same holes. `[?word]` partly supported by its figures, `[…]` not read,
`[65]` code not identified; the clear close in italics. Line markers as in the French.

## The siege

[f. 196r ends: *However, because the town is large and hard to besiege, it has been necessary to dig*]
[l. 1] trenches in several places. And being [l. 2] short of pioneers, the work has [l. 3] been the longer for it.

I believe that the day after tomorrow we shall [l. 4] begin to batter it with a battery of fifteen cannon, at
which [l. 5] it seems likely they will be taken aback, not being [l. 6] used to seeing places besieged in [l. 7] this
country with such an equipage.

Nevertheless, so far they [l. 8] make every show of wishing to [l. 9] defend themselves, so as not to suffer the
shame that the King of Navarre made [l. 10] the man who commanded in Sainte- [l. 11] Bazeille suffer, whom it is
certain he has had [l. 12] put in prison.

And if it is said that he means [l. 13] to do to him *pas* […] (the sentence breaks off: *faire pas* and then the
next sentence; perhaps *faire passer [le pas]*, "put to death", but the figures give only *pas*).

## Navarre and Turenne

We have word that he is to leave [l. 14] within a few days from [99] to go into [l. 15] Saintonge, and they
put it about among themselves that it is to [l. 16] gather […] of his friends, and from there to go to meet
[l. 17] his reiters.

The lord Viscount of Turenne is [l. 18] still at Nérac, from where there is no talk of his leaving; and it is
[l. 19] the common opinion that the charge of this whole [l. 20] province is to remain with him [l. 21] during the
absence of the said lord King of Navarre.

## The army's needs

Your Majesty has been informed [l. 22] by the earlier despatches, and still more [l. 23] [?p]articularly by the
sieur de [?Sénégal], [l. 24] of the needs of this army, as much for money as for [65], [l. 25] which grow worse every
day, so much that they can hardly [l. 26] be felt to grow any more, for they have almost [l. 27] reached the
extremity.

To which Your Majesty will provide, if it [l. 28] please you, because these [?could not] be remedied in an instant,
[l. 29] the evils which are [?afterwards] irreparable.

We await [l. 30] by the first despatch certainty about the [l. 31] sending of the third month['s pay], the waiting
for which is [l. 32] very costly to you, as Your Majesty will have learned from the last [l. 33] despatch.

And as it is also very necessary to make known [l. 34] promptly your will on the proposal [l. 35] which will be
made to you by [?Seval], concerning what is to be [l. 36] undertaken on leaving this siege.

## The clear close

*Sire, I most humbly pray the Creator to give you, in perfect health, a very long and very happy life. From the camp
before Monségur, the […] day of May 1586.*

*Your most humble, most obedient and most faithful —*

(The signature is not on the page.)

Notes. *Partement du troisième mois*: literally "the departure of the third month", i.e. the sending of the third
month's money for the army (the English adds "pay"). *Dont l'attente vous est bien chère*: "the waiting for which
costs you dear". *Que les ne se sent plus guères augmenter*: the cipher has *les* where *elles* is wanted; read as
"that they can hardly be felt to grow any more". *[?Seval]*: perhaps the sieur de Bosseval named in the clear
opening on f. 196r (the cipher gives only *seval*). *[99]*: the place Navarre is leaving from; *[65]*: a second
kind of supply beside money (provisions?).

f. 123r decoded, not read

Mayenne to the King from Marmande, March 1586, opening in clear ('la dernière que j'ay eu l'honneur est du huictiesme de ce moys'): no money, contributions to be had only by garrisons, the troops cannot be kept together.

A mechanical decode with holes in most sentences: shown so that the transcription and the key can be checked and improved, not as a reading.

# ff. 123r and 123v, fr. 15572: Brackx's transcription with the label corrections of corrections/f123.tsv
# Labels as in key_cipher1.json; leaf labels in key_cipher1_f123.json: Uv (V with underbar, e/u) and Cv (C with a
# v on its top right, s/x), the two figures Brackx merges as U; ssl (looped double s, ee/e); szl (open sharp s, ss);
# oob (barred double o, ff); ub (d with a crossed stem, u); dc = m; VMf (Vostre Majeste, from context); qt (q with
# a barred foot, t, context); cr (plain cross, c/e); del (struck figure, nothing); virg (slash, punctuation);
# q4 (crossed 4, unidentified); ff = n/o. f. 123r line 37 is not in Brackx's file and was transcribed here.
# One manuscript line per line.

Reading

reading_f123.md

# ff. 123r-v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: the reading

fr. 15572 f. 123r (Gallica canvas 129, right page) and f. 123v (canvas 130, left page). The despatch opens in clear,
then runs in Cipher-1 for 37 lines on f. 123r and 41 lines on f. 123v; it goes on over ff. 124r-v, which are read
in a later pass. No clear copy is known.

What was done, 22 Oct 2026: Michael Brackx's transcription was decoded with the merged key and then read sentence by
sentence against each figure's letter set. Every line of both pages was cropped from the full-resolution image
(openapi.bnf.fr IIIF) and the breaks were checked there. Brackx's f. 123r stops one line short: the last manuscript
line of the recto (l. 37) was transcribed here from the image. A second pass re-checked f. 123r line by line,
figure by figure, against an exemplar set cut from this hand (straightened line strips with every figure numbered;
crops kept outside the repository). The label changes, the corrected figures, the leaf key and the passes are listed
in the files named at the end. A third pass built an atlas of the hand (every figure of ff. 123r-124v cut and
labelled from the reading) and tested its shape classes on the open words; it adds *[?à satisfaire]* on l. 7 and
changes no key value.

Marks: a word in square brackets with a question mark is carried only in part by its figures; the ellipsis in
brackets is a run of figures that does not form a word; a number in brackets is a code group not identified; [UNK]
is a figure not in the key. Where a manuscript line breaks inside a word, the line marker stands before the word.
Accents and punctuation are modern; the spelling is what the figures give.

## The clear opening (f. 123r, five lines)

Sire, la dernière que j'ay [?escript] à Vostre Majesté est du huict[iesm]e de ce moys, et suis tousjours attendant
la responce des miennes des cinq[iesm]e, sept[iesm]e et dix[iesm]e de ces moys, estant estonné de me veoir réduict à
n'avoir de voz nouvelles que une foys par moys, comme je n'en ay pas eu tout cest hiver davantaige. Par toutes les
miennes depuis six sepmaines ou deux moys j'ay tousjours [?dépeint] […]

[The clear text was read from the image for context only. The last clear word, after *tousjours*, is written *d·*
then a p with a stroke, a dot, two minims and a hooked final stroke: *dépeint* fits it (*depuis*, written the same
way on the line, has no dot after the p); it stays marked. After a filler stroke the line ends with four signs that
are not in Brackx's file: a figure like *tt* joined to a C, a triple bar, and a crossed loop. They stand where the
sense wants *à Vostre Majesté* before cipher l. 1 *[UNK] la nécessité*, but no value is read from them.]

## The cipher, f. 123r

[l. 1] [UNK] la [?nécessité] que je [?prévois] où nous nous [l. 2] [?retrouverions] [16] vos armées se
[l. 3] [?reiondroient], laquele je [?trouve] [l. 4] maintenant beaucoup plus grande que je ne l'avois […],
[l. 5] commançant à sortir les clameurs et [l. 6] demandes de tous les gens de [?guerres], que l'on a […]
[l. 7] tousiours remis [?à satisfaire] […] [l. 8] [16] nous serions tous ensemble.

À quoy [l. 9] je suis si mal préparé que de plus de cent [l. 10] mile escus qu'il faudroict comptant, [l. 11] non pas
pour les paier d'un mois, mais pour [?optenir] [l. 12] d'eus ung peu de [?pacience], i vivre.

Et [?proteste] [l. 13] qu'il n'i en n'a pas douse mil escus ès mains [l. 14] du [?tresorier] de ceste armée.

Et si Monsieur [l. 15] le [?mareschal] de Matignon me mande qu'il a [l. 16] esté contrains d'emprunter plus de
[l. 17] vingt mil escus pour l'entretenement des [l. 18] [?forces] qui luy estoient demourés, [?lesquels] [l. 19] il faict estat que je face rembourcer à mon [l. 20] arrivée.

Je me voi en tele peine que je ne sai [l. 21] aucun moien d'en [?sortir].

Toute l'[66] a esté [l. 22] si [?harasée] de fatigues et de maladie, […] [l. 23] si mal vestue, que les mestres de
camp m'[?ont] [l. 24] [?tous] dict qu'il est impossible de s'en pouoir [l. 25] servir si l'on ne leur faict deux
[?monstres]; [l. 26] et [?protestent] qu'il n'[…] [?recevront] point [l. 27] pour une seule.

Les compaignies de [?gendarmes] [l. 28] qui sont icy n'i [?veulent] plus demourer [l. 29] sans faire monstre.

Les [101] et [102] sont [l. 30] encores plus [?importuns] que les autres; l'equipage [l. 31] qui [?est] desnu au
pire estat.

Quoy se [?peult] [l. 32] dire la nécessité des blés, si estreme [l. 33] que l'on n'[?en] peult avoir qu'à l'argent
comptant, [l. 34] et si seurement que nous n'i pouvons plus [l. 35] fournir.

Toutes les [?incommodités] [l. 36] [?m'arrivent] ensemble; [16] tous moiens me [l. 37] [?deffaillent] d'i
pourveoir, et lors que ceste

## The cipher, f. 123v

[l. 1] armée devoist estre plus employée et [l. 2] utile pour vostre service.

Je sai que [?messieurs] [l. 3] de vostre conseil sont ocupés en d'autres [l. 4] grans et importans affaires, qu'il
[l. 5] semble que cetuici, qui n'est pas des [?moindres], [l. 6] ne meritoist pas estre negligé, non [?plus] que les
autres; [l. 7] et serions tous blasmés, après avoir faict [l. 8] tant de despense de conserver ceste armée jusques
[l. 9] en ceste saison, de la laisser ainsi [?deperir] [l. 10] faulte d'[?entretenement].

Peut-estre ont-ils [l. 11] proposé à [?Vostre Majesté] que je puis tirer quelque bon secours [l. 12] de la
province où je sui, comme j'ai faict autrefois [l. 13] du Daulphiné quand j'y estois, et que […] [l. 14] de faire
encores [?quelques] autres provinces.

[l. 15] Mais ces exemples ne se peuuent maintenant [l. 16] [?rapporter] pour ceste-ci, où les enemis sont [l. 17] si
meslés parmi ce qui vous reste de bons subiects [l. 18] que persone n'habite la campaigne; et tout ce qu'ils [l. 19]
ont, ils sont contraints, contraints, de l'enclore [l. 20] pour se conserver eux-mesmes.

Comme [?je] croi, [l. 21] ils s'en aperçoivent bien au paiement de vos [l. 22] tailles, dont qu'ils trouueront le
[?recouvrement] [l. 23] très [?difficile].

De penser que l'on puise user [l. 24] de la forme de contribution comme font les enemis, [l. 25] je m'asseure que
teles violances ne seroient [l. 26] jamais approués de Vostre Majesté.

Mais quand elles furent [l. 27] [?promises], elles ne se peuuent executer que par [l. 28] les garnisons, et est
malaisé que les armes [l. 29] [?ne] puissent servir quand aux volontés de ceux [l. 30] [?du] païs.

Je confesse que je les ai trouués beaucoup [l. 31] plus froides que je [?ne] pensois. Mais il fault [?dire] [l. 32]
pour eux qu'ils ont aussi beaucoup plus pati que les autres, [l. 33] n'y aiant vile ni bourgade qui n'ait l'enemi
[l. 34] à une lieue près d'elle, où qu'ils sont encessamment [l. 35] à la guerre.

Aussi les moiens que j'attendois de [l. 36] Vostre [?costé] deffaillent. Ne pouant tirer d'ici [l. 37] aucun
secours, je suis contraint de [l. 38] recourir à [?Vostre Majesté] et vous depescher ce porteur exprès [l. 39]
pour vous supplier très [?humblement] me faire [l. 40] par lui entendre vostre commandement et entention,
[l. 41] et si […] desire se servir de ceste armée, f…

[The page ends here; the last word goes on to f. 124r.]

## Notes on the reading

f. 123r (second pass, figure by figure; changes in `brackx2026/corrections/f123r_v2.tsv`):
- l. 1: [UNK] is the first cipher figure, a cross with an o at each end of its bar; not in the key. *[?nécessité]*:
  the figures give *necesiti* (one s; the last figure is the ε-figure, i). *[?prévois]*: between *prevoi* and the s
  stands a double upright with a looped foot (label `Hl`), not the plain double cross that is c in *nécessité*,
  *clameurs*, *aucun*, *encores*; its value is not known.
- l. 2: *[?retrouverions]*: the figures give *retrouerions* (one u/v figure). Code 16 stands where *quand* (or *si*)
  is wanted here, at l. 8 (*[16] nous serions tous ensemble*) and at l. 36 (*[16] tous moiens me deffaillent*);
  read as a code, not adopted.
- l. 3: *[?reiondroient]* (*reiondroiet*, the n of *-ent* missing; the o is a slanted ff, not Hc); *[?trouve]*
  (*troue*: one u/v figure).
- l. 4: the six figures after *l'avois* are x, a long s (S, p/s: on the image it is the S of *plus*, not a
  virgule), e, t, the V with an underbar, the dotted v and B: *a p r e u n e*, no word.
- l. 6–7: *[?guerres]*: g u e rr s, no final e; the five figures after *que l'on* (Ca box ff dd m: *a u o s t*) do
  not make a word. l. 7–8: the figure Brackx writes oo2 (two o joined by a long bar) is the barred double o, ff, of
  *affaires* and *deffaillent* on f. 123v; with it the run after *remis* reads *a s a t ff a i r e e s a t ff r e a*.
  Third pass: the first nine figures are read *[?à satisfaire]* (*a sat·ffaire*: every figure carried, the *is* of
  *satisfaire* not written); the last eight (*e s a t ff r e a*) still do not form.
- l. 11: *[?optenir]*: *pour opt[e]nir*, one e short; the r of *pour* is a small hooked c.
- l. 12: *[?pacience]*: *p a c i e c e*, the n missing (the double cross is c, as everywhere on the page); the
  ε-figure after it is i, *i vivre* (*y vivre*).
- l. 12–13 *[?proteste]*: *protesse*, the small s-shaped figure where t is wanted; l. 14 *[?tresorier]*: the figure
  before the last *er* is the open C with an r inside (l), as in *qu'à l'argent* l. 33, not i; l. 15 *[?mareschal]*:
  *mar c a l*; l. 18 *[?forces]*, *[?lesquels]*: one e short each.
- ff = o on this leaf (as well as n): *retrouverions*, *reiondroient*, *tous ensemble*, *comptant*, *pouoir* (l. 25),
  *monstres*, *fournir* (l. 35).
- l. 21 *[?sortir]*: the crossed-stem figure (u) where s is wanted. *Toute l'[66] a esté*: the group is two 6 written
  together like 16 and 25 (Brackx: 6 B), a code whose value is not known (*armée*, *infanterie* fit the sense).
- l. 22 *[?harasée]*: a 4 with a looped stem (q4), not the crossed q, where h is wanted. l. 22–23: *maladie, […] si
  mal vestue*: the five figures before *si* are the heavy curled C (Cv, s/x; Brackx Cl), B, Ze, dd, m: *s e t s t*,
  no word; the C before *o* (*si*) is the same Cv.
- l. 23–24 *m'[?ont] [?tous]*: *mo t tos*, the figures checked (E, m, crossed q, dd): n and u are not written.
- l. 25–26 *[?monstres]*: after *deu-* stand the open C (l), dd, then *mon* + Cv t r dd V e t: *deul s monstr s
  et*, one figure too many and one e short. *[?protestent]*: *protetent* with a struck figure before the last t.
  *qu'il n'[…] [?recevront]*: v, 6, ff (*n i/n/s n/o*), then *r e c e r o n t*, one u/v figure short.
- l. 27–28 *[?gendarmes]*: *gend a m e s* (the first figure of l. 28 is an open a, not the cross), one r short;
  *[?veulent]* (*velent*); l. 30 *[?importuns]* (*impotus*); l. 31 *qui [?est]* (*quiet*); l. 31–32 *[?peult]*
  (*pelt*); l. 33 *n'[?en]* (*n i n*).
- l. 33 *qu'à l'argent comptant*: Brackx's *s y* is one group, the s-shaped 5 and a 9: code 59 = *argent*.
- l. 35–36 *[?incommodités] [?m'arrivent]*: the q with a barred foot is read as t from these two contexts only.
  *ensemble*: the last figure is the t-cross (e), not the 4.
- l. 36–37 *[?deffaillent]*: *d ff a i l e n t* (the first figure of l. 37 is the barred double o, ff). Codes 101
  and 102: two troop names, probably.

f. 123v:
- l. 6 *non [?plus]*: the figure for p is the barred o (a/m/i); l. 9–10 *[?deperir]*: the figures give *deper* + *i*
  + a cross; l. 10 *[?entretenement]*: the figures give *entretinemint* (an ε-figure twice where e is wanted).
- l. 11 and l. 38: one figure, a barred o crossed by a looped f-shaped stroke with a small o, stands where *Vostre
  Majesté* is wanted in both places (*proposé à [ ] que je puis*, *recourir à [ ] et vous depescher*); read as a
  code group from context, not from the key.
- l. 13–14 the two figure-groups after *et que* (*f o n t e o a s r e n t* by the sets) do not make words.
- l. 19 *contraints* is written twice (a slip of the encipherer).
- l. 22 *[?recouvrement]*: *le e e courement*, the third figure gives no r; l. 23 *[?difficile]*: *d i a i n/i s c i l*.
- l. 27 *[?promises]*: the second figure is x (e/a), not r. l. 31 *je [?ne] pensois*: the figures give *ient*.
- l. 36 *[?costé]*: *c o s t r s e*, two figures too many. l. 39 *[?humblement]*: the first figure is the long s/p
  figure, not h.
- l. 41 *et si […] desire*: one large curled figure, not identified (perhaps a code for the King).

Files: `brackx2026/corrections/f123.tsv` (label changes, both pages), `brackx2026/corrections/f123_cipher.txt`
(corrected figures, f. 123r ll. 1–37 then f. 123v ll. 1–41), `brackx2026/corrections/f123r_v2.tsv` and
`f123r_v2_cipher.txt` (second pass on f. 123r), `key_cipher1_f123.json` (leaf key),
`reading_f123_log.md` (passes), `reading_f123_en.md` (English).
Translation

reading_f123_en.md

# ff. 123r-v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: English translation

Translated from the reading (`reading_f123.md`), with the same holes. A word in square brackets with a question mark
translates a word the figures carry only in part; `[…]` is a run of figures that does not form a word; a number in
brackets is an unidentified code group; [UNK] a figure not in the key. Line numbers are those of the cipher on each
page.

## The clear opening (f. 123r)

Sire, the last letter I [?wrote] to Your Majesty is of the eighth of this month, and I am still awaiting the answer
to mine of the fifth, seventh and tenth of these months, being astonished to find myself reduced to having news from
you only once a month, as I have had no more all this winter. Through all my letters these six weeks or two months I
have always [?described] […]

## The cipher, f. 123r

[l. 1] [UNK] the [?necessity] in which I [?foresee] we would [l. 2] [?find] ourselves [16] your armies were to
[l. 3] [?join up again], which I [?find] [l. 4] now much greater than I had […], [l. 5] as the outcry and
[l. 6] demands of all the men of [?war] begin to break out, which have […] [l. 7] always been put off [?to be satisfied] […]
[l. 8] [16] we should all be together.

# ff. 123r-v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: the reading

fr. 15572 f. 123r (Gallica canvas 129, right page) and f. 123v (canvas 130, left page). The despatch opens in clear,
then runs in Cipher-1 for 37 lines on f. 123r and 41 lines on f. 123v; it goes on over ff. 124r-v, which are read
in a later pass. No clear copy is known.

What was done, 22 Oct 2026: Michael Brackx's transcription was decoded with the merged key and then read sentence by
sentence against each figure's letter set. Every line of both pages was cropped from the full-resolution image
(openapi.bnf.fr IIIF) and the breaks were checked there. Brackx's f. 123r stops one line short: the last manuscript
line of the recto (l. 37) was transcribed here from the image. A second pass re-checked f. 123r line by line,
figure by figure, against an exemplar set cut from this hand (straightened line strips with every figure numbered;
crops kept outside the repository). The label changes, the corrected figures, the leaf key and the passes are listed
in the files named at the end. A third pass built an atlas of the hand (every figure of ff. 123r-124v cut and
labelled from the reading) and tested its shape classes on the open words; it adds *[?à satisfaire]* on l. 7 and
changes no key value.

Marks: a word in square brackets with a question mark is carried only in part by its figures; the ellipsis in
brackets is a run of figures that does not form a word; a number in brackets is a code group not identified; [UNK]
is a figure not in the key. Where a manuscript line breaks inside a word, the line marker stands before the word.
Accents and punctuation are modern; the spelling is what the figures give.

## The clear opening (f. 123r, five lines)

Sire, la dernière que j'ay [?escript] à Vostre Majesté est du huict[iesm]e de ce moys, et suis tousjours attendant
la responce des miennes des cinq[iesm]e, sept[iesm]e et dix[iesm]e de ces moys, estant estonné de me veoir réduict à
n'avoir de voz nouvelles que une foys par moys, comme je n'en ay pas eu tout cest hiver davantaige. Par toutes les
miennes depuis six sepmaines ou deux moys j'ay tousjours [?dépeint] […]

[The clear text was read from the image for context only. The last clear word, after *tousjours*, is written *d·*
then a p with a stroke, a dot, two minims and a hooked final stroke: *dépeint* fits it (*depuis*, written the same
way on the line, has no dot after the p); it stays marked. After a filler stroke the line ends with four signs that
are not in Brackx's file: a figure like *tt* joined to a C, a triple bar, and a crossed loop. They stand where the
sense wants *à Vostre Majesté* before cipher l. 1 *[UNK] la nécessité*, but no value is read from them.]

## The cipher, f. 123r

[l. 1] [UNK] la [?nécessité] que je [?prévois] où nous nous [l. 2] [?retrouverions] [16] vos armées se
[l. 3] [?reiondroient], laquele je [?trouve] [l. 4] maintenant beaucoup plus grande que je ne l'avois […],
[l. 5] commançant à sortir les clameurs et [l. 6] demandes de tous les gens de [?guerres], que l'on a […]
[l. 7] tousiours remis [?à satisfaire] […] [l. 8] [16] nous serions tous ensemble.

À quoy [l. 9] je suis si mal préparé que de plus de cent [l. 10] mile escus qu'il faudroict comptant, [l. 11] non pas
pour les paier d'un mois, mais pour [?optenir] [l. 12] d'eus ung peu de [?pacience], i vivre.

Et [?proteste] [l. 13] qu'il n'i en n'a pas douse mil escus ès mains [l. 14] du [?tresorier] de ceste armée.

Et si Monsieur [l. 15] le [?mareschal] de Matignon me mande qu'il a [l. 16] esté contrains d'emprunter plus de
[l. 17] vingt mil escus pour l'entretenement des [l. 18] [?forces] qui luy estoient demourés, [?lesquels] [l. 19] il faict estat que je face rembourcer à mon [l. 20] arrivée.

Je me voi en tele peine que je ne sai [l. 21] aucun moien d'en [?sortir].

Toute l'[66] a esté [l. 22] si [?harasée] de fatigues et de maladie, […] [l. 23] si mal vestue, que les mestres de
camp m'[?ont] [l. 24] [?tous] dict qu'il est impossible de s'en pouoir [l. 25] servir si l'on ne leur faict deux
[?monstres]; [l. 26] et [?protestent] qu'il n'[…] [?recevront] point [l. 27] pour une seule.

Les compaignies de [?gendarmes] [l. 28] qui sont icy n'i [?veulent] plus demourer [l. 29] sans faire monstre.

Les [101] et [102] sont [l. 30] encores plus [?importuns] que les autres; l'equipage [l. 31] qui [?est] desnu au
pire estat.

Quoy se [?peult] [l. 32] dire la nécessité des blés, si estreme [l. 33] que l'on n'[?en] peult avoir qu'à l'argent
comptant, [l. 34] et si seurement que nous n'i pouvons plus [l. 35] fournir.

Toutes les [?incommodités] [l. 36] [?m'arrivent] ensemble; [16] tous moiens me [l. 37] [?deffaillent] d'i
pourveoir, et lors que ceste

## The cipher, f. 123v

[l. 1] armée devoist estre plus employée et [l. 2] utile pour vostre service.

Je sai que [?messieurs] [l. 3] de vostre conseil sont ocupés en d'autres [l. 4] grans et importans affaires, qu'il
[l. 5] semble que cetuici, qui n'est pas des [?moindres], [l. 6] ne meritoist pas estre negligé, non [?plus] que les
autres; [l. 7] et serions tous blasmés, après avoir faict [l. 8] tant de despense de conserver ceste armée jusques
[l. 9] en ceste saison, de la laisser ainsi [?deperir] [l. 10] faulte d'[?entretenement].

Peut-estre ont-ils [l. 11] proposé à [?Vostre Majesté] que je puis tirer quelque bon secours [l. 12] de la
province où je sui, comme j'ai faict autrefois [l. 13] du Daulphiné quand j'y estois, et que […] [l. 14] de faire
encores [?quelques] autres provinces.

[l. 15] Mais ces exemples ne se peuuent maintenant [l. 16] [?rapporter] pour ceste-ci, où les enemis sont [l. 17] si
meslés parmi ce qui vous reste de bons subiects [l. 18] que persone n'habite la campaigne; et tout ce qu'ils [l. 19]
ont, ils sont contraints, contraints, de l'enclore [l. 20] pour se conserver eux-mesmes.

Comme [?je] croi, [l. 21] ils s'en aperçoivent bien au paiement de vos [l. 22] tailles, dont qu'ils trouueront le
[?recouvrement] [l. 23] très [?difficile].

De penser que l'on puise user [l. 24] de la forme de contribution comme font les enemis, [l. 25] je m'asseure que
teles violances ne seroient [l. 26] jamais approués de Vostre Majesté.

Mais quand elles furent [l. 27] [?promises], elles ne se peuuent executer que par [l. 28] les garnisons, et est
malaisé que les armes [l. 29] [?ne] puissent servir quand aux volontés de ceux [l. 30] [?du] païs.

Je confesse que je les ai trouués beaucoup [l. 31] plus froides que je [?ne] pensois. Mais il fault [?dire] [l. 32]
pour eux qu'ils ont aussi beaucoup plus pati que les autres, [l. 33] n'y aiant vile ni bourgade qui n'ait l'enemi
[l. 34] à une lieue près d'elle, où qu'ils sont encessamment [l. 35] à la guerre.

Aussi les moiens que j'attendois de [l. 36] Vostre [?costé] deffaillent. Ne pouant tirer d'ici [l. 37] aucun
secours, je suis contraint de [l. 38] recourir à [?Vostre Majesté] et vous depescher ce porteur exprès [l. 39]
pour vous supplier très [?humblement] me faire [l. 40] par lui entendre vostre commandement et entention,
[l. 41] et si […] desire se servir de ceste armée, f…

[The page ends here; the last word goes on to f. 124r.]

## Notes on the reading

f. 123r (second pass, figure by figure; changes in `brackx2026/corrections/f123r_v2.tsv`):
- l. 1: [UNK] is the first cipher figure, a cross with an o at each end of its bar; not in the key. *[?nécessité]*:
  the figures give *necesiti* (one s; the last figure is the ε-figure, i). *[?prévois]*: between *prevoi* and the s
  stands a double upright with a looped foot (label `Hl`), not the plain double cross that is c in *nécessité*,
  *clameurs*, *aucun*, *encores*; its value is not known.
- l. 2: *[?retrouverions]*: the figures give *retrouerions* (one u/v figure). Code 16 stands where *quand* (or *si*)
  is wanted here, at l. 8 (*[16] nous serions tous ensemble*) and at l. 36 (*[16] tous moiens me deffaillent*);
  read as a code, not adopted.
- l. 3: *[?reiondroient]* (*reiondroiet*, the n of *-ent* missing; the o is a slanted ff, not Hc); *[?trouve]*
  (*troue*: one u/v figure).
- l. 4: the six figures after *l'avois* are x, a long s (S, p/s: on the image it is the S of *plus*, not a
  virgule), e, t, the V with an underbar, the dotted v and B: *a p r e u n e*, no word.
- l. 6–7: *[?guerres]*: g u e rr s, no final e; the five figures after *que l'on* (Ca box ff dd m: *a u o s t*) do
  not make a word. l. 7–8: the figure Brackx writes oo2 (two o joined by a long bar) is the barred double o, ff, of
  *affaires* and *deffaillent* on f. 123v; with it the run after *remis* reads *a s a t ff a i r e e s a t ff r e a*.
  Third pass: the first nine figures are read *[?à satisfaire]* (*a sat·ffaire*: every figure carried, the *is* of
  *satisfaire* not written); the last eight (*e s a t ff r e a*) still do not form.
- l. 11: *[?optenir]*: *pour opt[e]nir*, one e short; the r of *pour* is a small hooked c.
- l. 12: *[?pacience]*: *p a c i e c e*, the n missing (the double cross is c, as everywhere on the page); the
  ε-figure after it is i, *i vivre* (*y vivre*).
- l. 12–13 *[?proteste]*: *protesse*, the small s-shaped figure where t is wanted; l. 14 *[?tresorier]*: the figure
  before the last *er* is the open C with an r inside (l), as in *qu'à l'argent* l. 33, not i; l. 15 *[?mareschal]*:
  *mar c a l*; l. 18 *[?forces]*, *[?lesquels]*: one e short each.
- ff = o on this leaf (as well as n): *retrouverions*, *reiondroient*, *tous ensemble*, *comptant*, *pouoir* (l. 25),
  *monstres*, *fournir* (l. 35).
- l. 21 *[?sortir]*: the crossed-stem figure (u) where s is wanted. *Toute l'[66] a esté*: the group is two 6 written
  together like 16 and 25 (Brackx: 6 B), a code whose value is not known (*armée*, *infanterie* fit the sense).
- l. 22 *[?harasée]*: a 4 with a looped stem (q4), not the crossed q, where h is wanted. l. 22–23: *maladie, […] si
  mal vestue*: the five figures before *si* are the heavy curled C (Cv, s/x; Brackx Cl), B, Ze, dd, m: *s e t s t*,
  no word; the C before *o* (*si*) is the same Cv.
- l. 23–24 *m'[?ont] [?tous]*: *mo t tos*, the figures checked (E, m, crossed q, dd): n and u are not written.
- l. 25–26 *[?monstres]*: after *deu-* stand the open C (l), dd, then *mon* + Cv t r dd V e t: *deul s monstr s
  et*, one figure too many and one e short. *[?protestent]*: *protetent* with a struck figure before the last t.
  *qu'il n'[…] [?recevront]*: v, 6, ff (*n i/n/s n/o*), then *r e c e r o n t*, one u/v figure short.
- l. 27–28 *[?gendarmes]*: *gend a m e s* (the first figure of l. 28 is an open a, not the cross), one r short;
  *[?veulent]* (*velent*); l. 30 *[?importuns]* (*impotus*); l. 31 *qui [?est]* (*quiet*); l. 31–32 *[?peult]*
  (*pelt*); l. 33 *n'[?en]* (*n i n*).
- l. 33 *qu'à l'argent comptant*: Brackx's *s y* is one group, the s-shaped 5 and a 9: code 59 = *argent*.
- l. 35–36 *[?incommodités] [?m'arrivent]*: the q with a barred foot is read as t from these two contexts only.
  *ensemble*: the last figure is the t-cross (e), not the 4.
- l. 36–37 *[?deffaillent]*: *d ff a i l e n t* (the first figure of l. 37 is the barred double o, ff). Codes 101
  and 102: two troop names, probably.

f. 123v:
- l. 6 *non [?plus]*: the figure for p is the barred o (a/m/i); l. 9–10 *[?deperir]*: the figures give *deper* + *i*
  + a cross; l. 10 *[?entretenement]*: the figures give *entretinemint* (an ε-figure twice where e is wanted).
- l. 11 and l. 38: one figure, a barred o crossed by a looped f-shaped stroke with a small o, stands where *Vostre
  Majesté* is wanted in both places (*proposé à [ ] que je puis*, *recourir à [ ] et vous depescher*); read as a
  code group from context, not from the key.
- l. 13–14 the two figure-groups after *et que* (*f o n t e o a s r e n t* by the sets) do not make words.
- l. 19 *contraints* is written twice (a slip of the encipherer).
- l. 22 *[?recouvrement]*: *le e e courement*, the third figure gives no r; l. 23 *[?difficile]*: *d i a i n/i s c i l*.
- l. 27 *[?promises]*: the second figure is x (e/a), not r. l. 31 *je [?ne] pensois*: the figures give *ient*.
- l. 36 *[?costé]*: *c o s t r s e*, two figures too many. l. 39 *[?humblement]*: the first figure is the long s/p
  figure, not h.
- l. 41 *et si […] desire*: one large curled figure, not identified (perhaps a code for the King).

Files: `brackx2026/corrections/f123.tsv` (label changes, both pages), `brackx2026/corrections/f123_cipher.txt`
(corrected figures, f. 123r ll. 1–37 then f. 123v ll. 1–41), `brackx2026/corrections/f123r_v2.tsv` and
`f123r_v2_cipher.txt` (second pass on f. 123r), `key_cipher1_f123.json` (leaf key),
`reading_f123_log.md` (passes), `reading_f123_en.md` (English).

# ff. 123r-v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: English translation

Translated from the reading (`reading_f123.md`), with the same holes. A word in square brackets with a question mark
translates a word the figures carry only in part; `[…]` is a run of figures that does not form a word; a number in
brackets is an unidentified code group; [UNK] a figure not in the key. Line numbers are those of the cipher on each
page.

## The clear opening (f. 123r)

Sire, the last letter I [?wrote] to Your Majesty is of the eighth of this month, and I am still awaiting the answer
to mine of the fifth, seventh and tenth of these months, being astonished to find myself reduced to having news from
you only once a month, as I have had no more all this winter. Through all my letters these six weeks or two months I
have always [?described] […]

## The cipher, f. 123r

[l. 1] [UNK] the [?necessity] in which I [?foresee] we would [l. 2] [?find] ourselves [16] your armies were to
[l. 3] [?join up again], which I [?find] [l. 4] now much greater than I had […], [l. 5] as the outcry and
[l. 6] demands of all the men of [?war] begin to break out, which have […] [l. 7] always been put off [?to be satisfied] […]
[l. 8] [16] we should all be together.

For which [l. 9] I am so ill prepared that, of more than a hundred [l. 10] thousand écus that would be needed in
ready money, [l. 11] not to pay them for a month but to [?obtain] [l. 12] from them a little [?patience], to live there.

And [he] [?protests] [l. 13] that there are not twelve thousand écus in the hands [l. 14] of the [?treasurer] of
this army.

And if Monsieur [l. 15] the [?Marshal] de Matignon sends me word that he has [l. 16] been forced to borrow more than
[l. 17] twenty thousand écus for the upkeep of the [l. 18] [?forces] that remained to him, [?which] [l. 19] he counts on my having repaid on my [l. 20] arrival.

I find myself in such trouble that I know [l. 21] no way to [?get out] of it.

The whole [66] has been [l. 22] so [?worn out] with fatigue and sickness, […] [l. 23] so badly clothed, that the
camp-masters have [?all] told me [l. 24] it is impossible to make use of it [l. 25] unless two [?musters] are held for
them; [l. 26] and they [?protest] that they will not […] [?accept] [l. 27] for a single one.

The companies of [?men-at-arms] [l. 28] that are here will no longer stay [l. 29] without a muster being held.

The [101] and [102] are [l. 30] even more [?importunate] than the others; the baggage-train [l. 31] [?is] stripped bare, in the worst
state.

Besides this, [l. 32] one may speak of the shortage of grain, so extreme [l. 33] that one can [?get none] except for
ready money, [l. 34] and so surely that we can no longer [l. 35] supply it.

All the [?hardships] [l. 36] [?come upon me] together; [16] every means [l. 37] [?fails] me to provide for it, and
just when this

## The cipher, f. 123v

[l. 1] army ought to be more employed and [l. 2] useful for your service.

I know that [?the gentlemen] [l. 3] of your council are taken up with other [l. 4] great and important affairs, so
that it [l. 5] seems this one, which is not among the [?least], [l. 6] did not deserve to be neglected, [?any more]
than the others; [l. 7] and we would all be blamed if, after having gone to [l. 8] so much expense to keep this army
up until [l. 9] this season, we let it [?waste away] like this [l. 10] for want of [?upkeep].

Perhaps they have [l. 11] put it to [?Your Majesty] that I can draw some good help [l. 12] from the province I am
in, as I once did [l. 13] from the Dauphiné when I was there, and that […] [l. 14] to do the same again in
[?some] other provinces.

[l. 15] But those examples cannot now [l. 16] [?be applied] to this one, where the enemy are [l. 17] so mingled
among what remains to you of good subjects [l. 18] that no one lives in the open country; and all that they [l. 19]
have, they are forced (forced) to shut inside walls [l. 20] to keep themselves safe.

As [?I] believe, [l. 21] they are well aware of it in paying your [l. 22] taxes, the [?collection] of which they will
find [l. 23] very [?difficult].

As for thinking that one might use [l. 24] the form of contribution the enemy use, [l. 25] I am sure that such
violences would [l. 26] never be approved by Your Majesty.

And when they were [l. 27] [?promised], they could not be carried out except by [l. 28] the garrisons, and it is
hard for arms [l. 29] [?not] to serve the will of the people [l. 30] [?of the] country.

I confess that I have found them a great deal [l. 31] colder than I thought. But it must [?be said] [l. 32] for them
that they have also suffered much more than the others, [l. 33] there being no town or village that has not the
enemy [l. 34] within a league of it, so that they are ceaselessly [l. 35] at war.

So the means I was expecting from [l. 36] your [?side] are failing. Being unable to draw [l. 37] any help from here,
I am forced to [l. 38] have recourse to [?Your Majesty] and to send you this bearer expressly [l. 39] to beg you
most [?humbly] to let me [l. 40] know through him your command and intention, [l. 41] and if […] wishes to make use
of this army, …

[The page ends here; the last word goes on to f. 124r.]

The Marmande despatch continued: the bearer sent to learn the King's intention.

A mechanical decode with holes in most sentences: shown so that the transcription and the key can be checked and improved, not as a reading.

# ff. 124r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: the reading

fr. 15572 ff. 124r–124v, Gallica canvases 130 (right page) and 131 (left page). These two pages end the despatch
that opens in clear on f. 123r. f. 124r has 41 lines of Cipher-1 and f. 124v has 38. A clear postscript, the
closing and the dating line follow. The writer is the Duc de Mayenne with the army in Guyenne. He writes about
holding the army together, his money, the help of the Maréchal de Matignon, Gourgues and Forget, and the siege he
plans at Sainte-Bazeille.

What was done, 22 Oct 2026: Michael Brackx's transcription was decoded with the merged key. It was then read
sentence by sentence against each figure's letter set, and every line was checked on the full-resolution image.
Sixty-five labels were corrected and seven letter values were added for this leaf. A line-by-line forced alignment
of the reading onto the corrected figures was used as a check. The files named at the end list the corrections,
the leaf key and the passes. The beginning of the sentence on l. 1 is on f. 123v and is not read here.
Second pass on f. 124r (22 Oct 2026): every line was cut again along its own slope and compared figure by
figure with the labels; ten more label changes (`brackx2026/corrections/f124r_v2.tsv`) give *armée*, *sans
cela*, *je sai qu'elles*, *sur les lieus*, *nous a doné* and a candidate *[?faisant]* after code 60. f. 124v
is unchanged. Third pass (22 Oct 2026): an atlas of this hand (every figure of ff. 123r-124v cut and labelled from
the reading) shows the crossed oval and the tall crossed d, both labelled tm or th, as two shapes, mostly m and s;
l. 22 now reads *[?mieulx] estant* where the crossed oval had been taken as s (*si elles tant*). No key value changed.

Line numbers: a word divided between two lines stands after the marker of the line on which it ends.
Marks: a word in square brackets with a question mark is carried only in part by its figures. The ellipsis in
brackets is a run of figures that does not form a word. A bare number in brackets is a code group with no known
value: 60, 67 and `me` here; 68 is *Vostre Majesté* and 76 is *le Roy de Navarre*. Accents and punctuation are
modern. The spelling is what the figures give.

## f. 124r

[l. 1] […]aire quelque bone resolution de la secourir [l. 2] d'oresnavant plus souvent que l'en a esté. Car la
[l. 3] tenant en l'exercice où il la fault mettre, l'on ne [l. 4] sauroit [60] [?faisant] la retenir qu'en moins d'un
[l. 5] mois elle ne se deffase toute entière.

Ou si Vostre Majesté [l. 6] connoist ses moiens ne pouoir porter ceste [l. 7] despence, au moins si promptement
que le [?consi…] [l. 8] […], en attendant que lesdits moiens se [l. 9] puissent préparer, il luy plaira que
ladicte [l. 10] armée soit mise en garnison ès villes de ceste [l. 11] province, [?affin] que, la réduisant
d'une [l. 12] partie, je la meine en Rouergue ou [?Quercy], où il [l. 13] soffrent de la [?deffraier],
remettant la [l. 14] [?délivrance] de ceste faveur […] à une autre fois.

Où je ne suis venu [l. 15] que […] vostre [?espres] [l. 16] commandement et l'advis que m'en donnoict ledict
[l. 17] sieur marschal de Matignon, que sans cela [l. 18] l'armée [?qu'il] avoict près de luy estoict
[l. 19] toute ruinée.

Je fais, Sire, ceste proposicion [l. 20] très mal volontiers, parce que je sai [l. 21] qu'elles sont très
[?préjudiciables] à vostre service. Ce que [l. 22] je comprens, peult estre, [?mieulx] estant sur [l. 23] les
lieus […] que ceuls qui en [?parlent] de plus [l. 24] long.

Et que je sai que vos ennemis en tireront ung [l. 25] grant advantaige pour leur […].

[l. 26] Je vous puis dire que je veoi la pluspart de ceux de [l. 27] ceste armée prendre le peu de secours que l'on
nous a [l. 28] doné à si grande deffaveur que je [?crains] que cela leur [l. 29] diminue du courage qu'ils
avoient [?de] bien servir.

[l. 30] De ma part, je ne discontinueray point d'y faire mon [l. 31] devoir et tout l'[?effort] qu'il me sera
possible pour [l. 32] recouvrer moien de l'entretenir, attendant de vos nouvelles, [l. 33] m'aiant exposé
à [?vendre] et engaiger tout le bien que [l. 34] j'ay icy pour faire argent pour y emploier. Et faict venir
[l. 35] [?aussi] monsieur de Gourgues pour nous aider de son [l. 36] crédit, affin d'emprunter à interest et à
[l. 37] change tout ce qu'il pourra [?recouvrer] […].

J'ay aussi [l. 38] résolu d'envoier veoir [?ceulx] du [?parlement] et [l. 39] les jurats de [?ladite] ville pour
les [?exhorter] [l. 40] de quoy faire, à l'[?exemple] de ceulx de [l. 41] [?Toulouse], […] de […]
province, […] [?quelque] [f. 124v, l. 1] secors, puisqu'ils doivent recevoir les [l. 2] présens fruicts de
ceste armée.

## f. 124v

Et [?combien] [l. 3] que Monsieur Forget me soict icy fort [l. 4] [?nécessaire], toutesfois, n'en aiant
point de plus propre pour [l. 5] traicter de cela avec eulx, je me suis [l. 6] contrains de luy en […] tant
pour ce faict que [l. 7] pour, avec ledict sieur de Gourgues, [l. 8] emprunter et argent et draps le plus qu'il
pourront.

Car je [l. 9] veoi [?qu'il] n'y a ordre [60] faire monstre aux gens de pied, [l. 10] de les pouoir faire camper
comme il fault qu'il [l. 11] facent au premier siège.

Sire, [?je] vous escrivis du [l. 12] vingt [?uniesme], partant de Villeneufve [l. 13] d'Agenois. Depuis je suis
venu à [?Thonains], que les [l. 14] ennemis ont habandonné, [?et] j'ay laissé [l. 15] garnison pour garder ce
passaige, et suis venu en [l. 16] ceste ville.

Et aiant eu advis de mondict [l. 17] sieur le mareschal qu'il [?est], avec ce qu'il a [l. 18] emporté,
[?Castels], et au plus tard […] [l. 19] Pasques, [?je] me suis par mon advis résolu [l. 20] [?d'aller] attacquer
Saincte Basille sitost qu'il [l. 21] m'aura envoié cinq canons et la pouldre [l. 22] et les balles qui me sont
nécessaires, comme il [l. 23] m'a [?mandé] qu'il feroit.

Je [?seré] avec cela, [l. 24] et si je puis trouve[r] moien de faire [l. 25] monstre aux gens de guerre, que comme
tost nous les [l. 26] porterons, ainsi qu'ils l'aient faict remparer et [l. 27] qu'il y ait bon nombre de gens [de]
guerre [l. 28] dedans, comme il fault confesser que toutes leurs [l. 29] places en sont bien munies.

Nous n'avons point [l. 30] encores nouvelles qu'on ait repassé la [?Dordogne], [l. 31] et estime qu'il soit
tousiours à [l. 32] Bergerac.

Le vicomte de Turenne est venu à Nérac avec quelque [67]. Je croy [l. 33] que ce soit plus en intention de
repasser [l. 34] la Garonne et suivre ledict sieur [76] que de [l. 35] séjourner icy, où je le désirerois mieux
que [l. 36] [?ailleurs], car ce seroit de l'exercice pour [l. 37] nostre [67] pendant que nous serions […]
[l. 38] audict siège [me].

## The clear end (another hand)

T. Qui est tout ce […] que de bonne […] pour cest effect.

Sire, je prie Dieu donner à Vostre Majesté en très parfaicte santé très heureuse et très longue vie. Du camp de
Marmande, ce […] mars 1586.

## What stays open

- **Codes with no value:** 60 (twice: f. 124r l. 4 and f. 124v l. 9, "ordre [60] faire monstre", where *de* or
  *pour* would fit), 67 (twice: "avec quelque [67]", "pour nostre [67]"; a word like *troupe* fits both), `me`
  (f. 124v l. 38).
- **Figures that do not make the word** (each one was checked on the image): l. 4 the word after code 60 (*[?faisant]*: the first figure is the f of f. 110, not yet seen elsewhere on this leaf);
  l. 7–8 the word after *que le*, which goes over a three-figure interlinear insertion whose place is not certain;
  l. 11 *[?affin]* (crossed 4, slashed q, dotted cross: not the letters *a ff*); l. 12 *[?Quercy]* (q u e/r c i,
  one figure short); l. 13–14 *[?deffraier]* and *[?délivrance]* (the A figure gives l, not n); l. 15 the four
  figures before *vostre*; l. 21 *[?préjudiciables]* (no r and only one i in the figures); l. 23 the crossed-d figure after *lieus*; l. 18 the figure after *qui*, which is a blot; l. 25 the end of the line; l. 37 the eleven figures before *ai aussi*; l. 41 the two words
  around *province*. On f. 124v: l. 2 *[?combien]* (a y where b is needed); l. 6 the verb after *luy en*; l. 12
  *[?uniesme]*; l. 18 the place-name (*[?Castels]*) and the words before *Pasques*; l. 23 *[?mandé]* and
  *[?seré]*; l. 30 the river (*d o r d …*); l. 37 the participle before *audict siège*.
- **The clear end** is in a small cursive hand, and two words in it and the day of the month were not read from
  the image at this resolution.

Files: `brackx2026/corrections/f124.tsv` (label changes), `brackx2026/corrections/f124r_v2.tsv` and
`f124r_v2_cipher.txt` (f. 124r, second pass), `brackx2026/corrections/f124_cipher.txt` (corrected
figures, f. 124r then f. 124v), `key_cipher1_f124.json` (leaf key), `reading_f124_log.md` (passes),
`reading_f124_en.md` (English).

# ff. 124r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: English translation

A translation of `reading_f124.md`, which ends the despatch that opens in clear on f. 123r. It keeps the same holes
and the same line markers. A word marked [?] is only partly carried by its figures, and […] is a run of figures that
does not form a word. [60] and [67] are code groups with no known value. [76] is the code for the King of Navarre.

## f. 124r

[l. 1] […] some good resolution to relieve it [l. 2] from now on more often than has been done. For, keeping
[l. 3] it at the work where it has to be put, one could [l. 4] not, [60] [?doing so], hold it together; within less than a
[l. 5] month it would fall apart entirely.

Or, if Your Majesty [l. 6] knows that his means cannot bear this [l. 7] expense, at least not as promptly as
[?consi…] [l. 8] […], then until the said means can [l. 9] be got ready it will please him that the said [l. 10]
army be put into garrison in the towns of this [l. 11] province, [?so] that, reducing it in [l. 12] part, I
may take it into the Rouergue or the [?Quercy], where they [l. 13] put up with [?paying its costs], putting off
the [l. 14] [?granting] of this favour […] to another time.

Where I came only [l. 15] by […] your [?express] [l. 16] command and on the advice that the said [l. 17] lord
Marshal de Matignon gave me about it: that without that [l. 18] the army [?he] had with him was [l. 19] wholly
ruined.

I make this proposal, Sire, [l. 20] very unwillingly, because I know [l. 21] that they are very
[?prejudicial] to your service. This I [l. 22] understand perhaps [?better], being on [l. 23] the spot […], than those who speak of it from [l. 24] further off.

And because I know that your enemies will draw from it a [l. 25] great advantage for their […].

[l. 26] I can tell you that I see most of those in [l. 27] this army take the little help that has been [l. 28] given us so badly that I [?fear] it will lessen [l. 29] the courage they had [?to] serve well.

[l. 30] For my part, I will not stop doing my [l. 31] duty here, and every [?effort] that I can make to
[l. 32] find the means of keeping it up while waiting for news from you, [l. 33] having put myself to [?selling]
and pledging all the property that [l. 34] I have here to raise money to spend on it. I have also had
[l. 35] Monsieur de Gourgues come [?as well], to help us with his [l. 36] credit, so as to borrow at interest
and on [l. 37] exchange everything he can [?recover] […].

I have also [l. 38] resolved to send to see [?those] of the [?parlement] and [l. 39] the jurats of [?the said]
town, to [?exhort] them [l. 40] to provide the means, after the [?example] of those of [l. 41] [?Toulouse], […]
of […] province, […] [?some] [f. 124v, l. 1] relief, since they are to receive the [l. 2] present fruits of this
army.

## f. 124v

And [?although] [l. 3] Monsieur Forget is very [l. 4] [?necessary] to me here, nevertheless, having no one fitter
to [l. 5] deal with them about it, I have [l. 6] been forced to […] him with it, both for this business and
[l. 7] so that, with the said sieur de Gourgues, [l. 8] he may borrow both money and cloth, as much as they can.

For I [l. 9] see [?that] there is no order [60] holding a muster of the foot, [l. 10] so as to be able to make them
camp as they must [l. 11] at the first siege.

Sire, [?I] wrote to you on the [l. 12] twenty-[?first], leaving Villeneuve [l. 13] d'Agenais. Since then I have
come to [?Tonneins], which the [l. 14] enemy have abandoned, [?and] I have left a [l. 15] garrison to guard this
crossing, and I have come into [l. 16] this town.

And having had word from my said [l. 17] lord the Marshal that he [?is], with what he has [l. 18] taken,
[?Castets], and at the latest […] [l. 19] Easter, [?I] have resolved on my own judgment [l. 20] to [?go] and attack
Sainte-Bazeille as soon as he [l. 21] has sent me five cannon and the powder [l. 22] and the shot I need, as he
[l. 23] has [?told] me he would.

I [?shall be] with that, [l. 24] and if I can find the means to hold a [l. 25] muster of the soldiers, as soon
as we carry the places, [l. 26] even though they have had them ramparted and [l. 27] there is a good number of
soldiers [l. 28] inside; for it must be admitted that all their [l. 29] places are well furnished with them.

We have no [l. 30] news yet that anyone has crossed back over the [?Dordogne], [l. 31] and I reckon he is still at
[l. 32] Bergerac.

The Vicomte de Turenne has come to Nérac with some [67]. I believe [l. 33] it is more with the intention of
crossing back over [l. 34] the Garonne and following the said lord [76, the King of Navarre] than of [l. 35]
staying here, where I would rather have him than [l. 36] [?elsewhere], for it would be exercise for [l. 37] our
[67] while we were […] [l. 38] at the said siege [me].

## The clear end (another hand)

T. Which is all the […] than of good […] for this purpose.

Sire, I pray God to give Your Majesty, in very perfect health, a very happy and very long life. From the camp at
Marmande, this […] March 1586.

Reading

reading_f124.md

# ff. 124r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: the reading

fr. 15572 ff. 124r–124v, Gallica canvases 130 (right page) and 131 (left page). These two pages end the despatch
that opens in clear on f. 123r. f. 124r has 41 lines of Cipher-1 and f. 124v has 38. A clear postscript, the
closing and the dating line follow. The writer is the Duc de Mayenne with the army in Guyenne. He writes about
holding the army together, his money, the help of the Maréchal de Matignon, Gourgues and Forget, and the siege he
plans at Sainte-Bazeille.

What was done, 22 Oct 2026: Michael Brackx's transcription was decoded with the merged key. It was then read
sentence by sentence against each figure's letter set, and every line was checked on the full-resolution image.
Sixty-five labels were corrected and seven letter values were added for this leaf. A line-by-line forced alignment
of the reading onto the corrected figures was used as a check. The files named at the end list the corrections,
the leaf key and the passes. The beginning of the sentence on l. 1 is on f. 123v and is not read here.
Second pass on f. 124r (22 Oct 2026): every line was cut again along its own slope and compared figure by
figure with the labels; ten more label changes (`brackx2026/corrections/f124r_v2.tsv`) give *armée*, *sans
cela*, *je sai qu'elles*, *sur les lieus*, *nous a doné* and a candidate *[?faisant]* after code 60. f. 124v
is unchanged. Third pass (22 Oct 2026): an atlas of this hand (every figure of ff. 123r-124v cut and labelled from
the reading) shows the crossed oval and the tall crossed d, both labelled tm or th, as two shapes, mostly m and s;
l. 22 now reads *[?mieulx] estant* where the crossed oval had been taken as s (*si elles tant*). No key value changed.

Line numbers: a word divided between two lines stands after the marker of the line on which it ends.
Marks: a word in square brackets with a question mark is carried only in part by its figures. The ellipsis in
brackets is a run of figures that does not form a word. A bare number in brackets is a code group with no known
value: 60, 67 and `me` here; 68 is *Vostre Majesté* and 76 is *le Roy de Navarre*. Accents and punctuation are
modern. The spelling is what the figures give.

## f. 124r

[l. 1] […]aire quelque bone resolution de la secourir [l. 2] d'oresnavant plus souvent que l'en a esté. Car la
[l. 3] tenant en l'exercice où il la fault mettre, l'on ne [l. 4] sauroit [60] [?faisant] la retenir qu'en moins d'un
[l. 5] mois elle ne se deffase toute entière.

Ou si Vostre Majesté [l. 6] connoist ses moiens ne pouoir porter ceste [l. 7] despence, au moins si promptement
que le [?consi…] [l. 8] […], en attendant que lesdits moiens se [l. 9] puissent préparer, il luy plaira que
ladicte [l. 10] armée soit mise en garnison ès villes de ceste [l. 11] province, [?affin] que, la réduisant
d'une [l. 12] partie, je la meine en Rouergue ou [?Quercy], où il [l. 13] soffrent de la [?deffraier],
remettant la [l. 14] [?délivrance] de ceste faveur […] à une autre fois.

Où je ne suis venu [l. 15] que […] vostre [?espres] [l. 16] commandement et l'advis que m'en donnoict ledict
[l. 17] sieur marschal de Matignon, que sans cela [l. 18] l'armée [?qu'il] avoict près de luy estoict
[l. 19] toute ruinée.

Je fais, Sire, ceste proposicion [l. 20] très mal volontiers, parce que je sai [l. 21] qu'elles sont très
[?préjudiciables] à vostre service. Ce que [l. 22] je comprens, peult estre, [?mieulx] estant sur [l. 23] les
lieus […] que ceuls qui en [?parlent] de plus [l. 24] long.

Et que je sai que vos ennemis en tireront ung [l. 25] grant advantaige pour leur […].

[l. 26] Je vous puis dire que je veoi la pluspart de ceux de [l. 27] ceste armée prendre le peu de secours que l'on
nous a [l. 28] doné à si grande deffaveur que je [?crains] que cela leur [l. 29] diminue du courage qu'ils
avoient [?de] bien servir.

[l. 30] De ma part, je ne discontinueray point d'y faire mon [l. 31] devoir et tout l'[?effort] qu'il me sera
possible pour [l. 32] recouvrer moien de l'entretenir, attendant de vos nouvelles, [l. 33] m'aiant exposé
à [?vendre] et engaiger tout le bien que [l. 34] j'ay icy pour faire argent pour y emploier. Et faict venir
[l. 35] [?aussi] monsieur de Gourgues pour nous aider de son [l. 36] crédit, affin d'emprunter à interest et à
[l. 37] change tout ce qu'il pourra [?recouvrer] […].

J'ay aussi [l. 38] résolu d'envoier veoir [?ceulx] du [?parlement] et [l. 39] les jurats de [?ladite] ville pour
les [?exhorter] [l. 40] de quoy faire, à l'[?exemple] de ceulx de [l. 41] [?Toulouse], […] de […]
province, […] [?quelque] [f. 124v, l. 1] secors, puisqu'ils doivent recevoir les [l. 2] présens fruicts de
ceste armée.

## f. 124v

Et [?combien] [l. 3] que Monsieur Forget me soict icy fort [l. 4] [?nécessaire], toutesfois, n'en aiant
point de plus propre pour [l. 5] traicter de cela avec eulx, je me suis [l. 6] contrains de luy en […] tant
pour ce faict que [l. 7] pour, avec ledict sieur de Gourgues, [l. 8] emprunter et argent et draps le plus qu'il
pourront.

Car je [l. 9] veoi [?qu'il] n'y a ordre [60] faire monstre aux gens de pied, [l. 10] de les pouoir faire camper
comme il fault qu'il [l. 11] facent au premier siège.

Sire, [?je] vous escrivis du [l. 12] vingt [?uniesme], partant de Villeneufve [l. 13] d'Agenois. Depuis je suis
venu à [?Thonains], que les [l. 14] ennemis ont habandonné, [?et] j'ay laissé [l. 15] garnison pour garder ce
passaige, et suis venu en [l. 16] ceste ville.

Et aiant eu advis de mondict [l. 17] sieur le mareschal qu'il [?est], avec ce qu'il a [l. 18] emporté,
[?Castels], et au plus tard […] [l. 19] Pasques, [?je] me suis par mon advis résolu [l. 20] [?d'aller] attacquer
Saincte Basille sitost qu'il [l. 21] m'aura envoié cinq canons et la pouldre [l. 22] et les balles qui me sont
nécessaires, comme il [l. 23] m'a [?mandé] qu'il feroit.

Je [?seré] avec cela, [l. 24] et si je puis trouve[r] moien de faire [l. 25] monstre aux gens de guerre, que comme
tost nous les [l. 26] porterons, ainsi qu'ils l'aient faict remparer et [l. 27] qu'il y ait bon nombre de gens [de]
guerre [l. 28] dedans, comme il fault confesser que toutes leurs [l. 29] places en sont bien munies.

Nous n'avons point [l. 30] encores nouvelles qu'on ait repassé la [?Dordogne], [l. 31] et estime qu'il soit
tousiours à [l. 32] Bergerac.

Le vicomte de Turenne est venu à Nérac avec quelque [67]. Je croy [l. 33] que ce soit plus en intention de
repasser [l. 34] la Garonne et suivre ledict sieur [76] que de [l. 35] séjourner icy, où je le désirerois mieux
que [l. 36] [?ailleurs], car ce seroit de l'exercice pour [l. 37] nostre [67] pendant que nous serions […]
[l. 38] audict siège [me].

## The clear end (another hand)

T. Qui est tout ce […] que de bonne […] pour cest effect.

Sire, je prie Dieu donner à Vostre Majesté en très parfaicte santé très heureuse et très longue vie. Du camp de
Marmande, ce […] mars 1586.

## What stays open

- **Codes with no value:** 60 (twice: f. 124r l. 4 and f. 124v l. 9, "ordre [60] faire monstre", where *de* or
  *pour* would fit), 67 (twice: "avec quelque [67]", "pour nostre [67]"; a word like *troupe* fits both), `me`
  (f. 124v l. 38).
- **Figures that do not make the word** (each one was checked on the image): l. 4 the word after code 60 (*[?faisant]*: the first figure is the f of f. 110, not yet seen elsewhere on this leaf);
  l. 7–8 the word after *que le*, which goes over a three-figure interlinear insertion whose place is not certain;
  l. 11 *[?affin]* (crossed 4, slashed q, dotted cross: not the letters *a ff*); l. 12 *[?Quercy]* (q u e/r c i,
  one figure short); l. 13–14 *[?deffraier]* and *[?délivrance]* (the A figure gives l, not n); l. 15 the four
  figures before *vostre*; l. 21 *[?préjudiciables]* (no r and only one i in the figures); l. 23 the crossed-d figure after *lieus*; l. 18 the figure after *qui*, which is a blot; l. 25 the end of the line; l. 37 the eleven figures before *ai aussi*; l. 41 the two words
  around *province*. On f. 124v: l. 2 *[?combien]* (a y where b is needed); l. 6 the verb after *luy en*; l. 12
  *[?uniesme]*; l. 18 the place-name (*[?Castels]*) and the words before *Pasques*; l. 23 *[?mandé]* and
  *[?seré]*; l. 30 the river (*d o r d …*); l. 37 the participle before *audict siège*.
- **The clear end** is in a small cursive hand, and two words in it and the day of the month were not read from
  the image at this resolution.

`f124r_v2_cipher.txt` (f. 124r, second pass), `brackx2026/corrections/f124_cipher.txt` (corrected

reading_f124_en.md

# ff. 124r–v, Mayenne to Henri III, Marmande, March 1586: English translation

A translation of `reading_f124.md`, which ends the despatch that opens in clear on f. 123r. It keeps the same holes
and the same line markers. A word marked [?] is only partly carried by its figures, and […] is a run of figures that
does not form a word. [60] and [67] are code groups with no known value. [76] is the code for the King of Navarre.

## f. 124r

[l. 1] […] some good resolution to relieve it [l. 2] from now on more often than has been done. For, keeping
[l. 3] it at the work where it has to be put, one could [l. 4] not, [60] [?doing so], hold it together; within less than a
[l. 5] month it would fall apart entirely.

Or, if Your Majesty [l. 6] knows that his means cannot bear this [l. 7] expense, at least not as promptly as
[?consi…] [l. 8] […], then until the said means can [l. 9] be got ready it will please him that the said [l. 10]
army be put into garrison in the towns of this [l. 11] province, [?so] that, reducing it in [l. 12] part, I
may take it into the Rouergue or the [?Quercy], where they [l. 13] put up with [?paying its costs], putting off
the [l. 14] [?granting] of this favour […] to another time.

Where I came only [l. 15] by […] your [?express] [l. 16] command and on the advice that the said [l. 17] lord
Marshal de Matignon gave me about it: that without that [l. 18] the army [?he] had with him was [l. 19] wholly
ruined.

I make this proposal, Sire, [l. 20] very unwillingly, because I know [l. 21] that they are very
[?prejudicial] to your service. This I [l. 22] understand perhaps [?better], being on [l. 23] the spot […], than those who speak of it from [l. 24] further off.

And because I know that your enemies will draw from it a [l. 25] great advantage for their […].

[l. 26] I can tell you that I see most of those in [l. 27] this army take the little help that has been [l. 28] given us so badly that I [?fear] it will lessen [l. 29] the courage they had [?to] serve well.

[l. 30] For my part, I will not stop doing my [l. 31] duty here, and every [?effort] that I can make to
[l. 32] find the means of keeping it up while waiting for news from you, [l. 33] having put myself to [?selling]
and pledging all the property that [l. 34] I have here to raise money to spend on it. I have also had
[l. 35] Monsieur de Gourgues come [?as well], to help us with his [l. 36] credit, so as to borrow at interest
and on [l. 37] exchange everything he can [?recover] […].

I have also [l. 38] resolved to send to see [?those] of the [?parlement] and [l. 39] the jurats of [?the said]
town, to [?exhort] them [l. 40] to provide the means, after the [?example] of those of [l. 41] [?Toulouse], […]
of […] province, […] [?some] [f. 124v, l. 1] relief, since they are to receive the [l. 2] present fruits of this
army.

## f. 124v

And [?although] [l. 3] Monsieur Forget is very [l. 4] [?necessary] to me here, nevertheless, having no one fitter
to [l. 5] deal with them about it, I have [l. 6] been forced to […] him with it, both for this business and
[l. 7] so that, with the said sieur de Gourgues, [l. 8] he may borrow both money and cloth, as much as they can.

For I [l. 9] see [?that] there is no order [60] holding a muster of the foot, [l. 10] so as to be able to make them
camp as they must [l. 11] at the first siege.

Sire, [?I] wrote to you on the [l. 12] twenty-[?first], leaving Villeneuve [l. 13] d'Agenais. Since then I have
come to [?Tonneins], which the [l. 14] enemy have abandoned, [?and] I have left a [l. 15] garrison to guard this
crossing, and I have come into [l. 16] this town.

And having had word from my said [l. 17] lord the Marshal that he [?is], with what he has [l. 18] taken,
[?Castets], and at the latest […] [l. 19] Easter, [?I] have resolved on my own judgment [l. 20] to [?go] and attack
Sainte-Bazeille as soon as he [l. 21] has sent me five cannon and the powder [l. 22] and the shot I need, as he
[l. 23] has [?told] me he would.

I [?shall be] with that, [l. 24] and if I can find the means to hold a [l. 25] muster of the soldiers, as soon
as we carry the places, [l. 26] even though they have had them ramparted and [l. 27] there is a good number of
soldiers [l. 28] inside; for it must be admitted that all their [l. 29] places are well furnished with them.

We have no [l. 30] news yet that anyone has crossed back over the [?Dordogne], [l. 31] and I reckon he is still at
[l. 32] Bergerac.

The Vicomte de Turenne has come to Nérac with some [67]. I believe [l. 33] it is more with the intention of
crossing back over [l. 34] the Garonne and following the said lord [76, the King of Navarre] than of [l. 35]
staying here, where I would rather have him than [l. 36] [?elsewhere], for it would be exercise for [l. 37] our
[67] while we were […] [l. 38] at the said siege [me].

## The clear end (another hand)

T. Which is all the […] than of good […] for this purpose.

Sire, I pray God to give Your Majesty, in very perfect health, a very happy and very long life. From the camp at
Marmande, this […] March 1586.

f. 110 read in part

Mayenne to Villeroy from Villeneuve-d'Agenais, March 1586: on making war without money and what it does to his reputation; the leaf is wholly in cipher.

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (94% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

# fr. 15572 f. 110, Mayenne to Villeroy, Bellebourg, March 1586: decryption with the merged Cipher-1 key (key_cipher1.json)
# Transcription: Michael Brackx (issues #73, #77-#79, #83), brackx2026/. Decoded here with this
# project's beam and period-French 6-gram (final_texts.py). Per manuscript line: the figures, then the
# decode cut into words. UPPER CASE = inside a run of French (measure.py sense rule); |lower case| =
# outside one (doubtful letters, unknown spellings, slips); [nn] = code group not identified.
# Measured: 1342 of 2164 tokens read as sense (62.0%); scrambled-key floor 19.3%.

    |oo i uie ur de uile e roi uous uerre a pies| PAR LA METTRE QUE SE |fasseu| ROI DUR

reading_f110.md

# f. 110, Mayenne to Villeroy, Villeneuve-d'Agenais, March 1586: the reading

fr. 15572 f. 110, Gallica canvas 116, right page: a small slip of thirty-eight lines wholly in cipher, in a small and
faded hand, dated at the foot in clear. Mayenne writes to Villeroy, the King's secretary of state, about the army he
commands in Guyenne against the King of Navarre: no money, the promised funds not sent, the clergy's money held
back, and his reputation at stake if the army is left to fall apart. No clear copy of this leaf is known.

What was done, 22 Oct 2026, in three passes. The transcription by Michael Brackx was decoded with the merged key and
read sentence by sentence against each figure's letter set. Then the whole leaf was transcribed again figure by
figure from straightened crops of the full-resolution image, against a set of exemplars cut from this hand. In the
third pass every remaining break was cropped again and read against the sets; eight more labels were corrected and
one figure was added to the leaf key (a tilde with a looped tail, read as s in its two places). The leaf is read in
part: the frame of the letter and its argument hold, and lines 1-2, 10, 14 and 24-25 keep holes.

The dateline reads on the crop: De Villeneufve d'Agenoys, ce … jour de Mars 1586.

Marks: a word in square brackets with a question mark is carried only in part by its figures; the ellipsis in
brackets is a run of figures that does not form a word; [45] and [47] are code groups (47 is tous or tout where the
context settles it). Accents and punctuation are modern; the spelling is what the figures give (*ce* for *se*,
*-mant* for *-ment*, *tousiour*).

## The cipher

[l. 1] [?Monsieur] de [?Villeroy],

vous verres bien par la lettre que ie fais au roi du r[…] [l. 2] […] [?retenu] de […] le plus […] que ie […]
[l. 3] [?reservés] de [?parler] plus librement avecq vous, avec ce qu'avait tousiour faict [l. 4] [?estat] du
grande [?confidence] et de particulière amitié, i'ai [?recongn][l. 5]u dès le commancement.

Pour le peu de […] que i'ai veu avoir de ceste [l. 6] armée, qu'elle estoit un peu de [?considération], et que l'on
ne s'attendoit pas [l. 7] beaucoup de bons effects, […] plus particulièrement remarqué en ce trai[l. 8]ct qui est
dans vostre dernière depesche, que le roi a pris […] [l. 9] dangiers qui arrivent à ceux qui entreprennent la
guerre sans argen[l. 10]t.

Comme il a faict […] [45]ssement, que [47] […] paroles, il n'a eu […] [l. 11] plus de substance.

Retenue qu'il s'en paroist. Il fault parler clairement.

[l. 12] Si le roi est ennuié de la guerre, et qu'il la veuile finir, il ce peult fa[l. 13]ire plus [?honnestement]
que par la ruine de ma reputation, et que de m'exposer [l. 14] pour presser que la guerre […] [?suive], […] que
l'en […] ce peust soustenir [l. 15] […] le roi de Navarre [45] [?ses] ennemis qui l'en veullent, puis aider ce
moien.

Car [l. 16] leur argument peult faillir.

Au reste ie puis dire que […] ceste armée [l. 17] n'a faict de meilleurs [?effects], qu'il n'i a poinct de sa faulte,
qu'au contraire [l. 18] qu'il ne se pouvoit mieux faire que de la conserver, comme i'ai faict, [l. 19] avecque peu
de despence, pour s'en servir à la saison et aux occasions qui [l. 20] [?s'offrent] maintenant.

Quand à la guerre, elle se peult faire, et utillemant, [l. 21] si [?tost] se veult tenir promesse d'envoier les
deniers qui ont esté destin[l. 22]ez, à quoi l'empeschemant de ceux du clergé n'est poinct contraire.

Mais si [l. 23] sa Maiesté iuge meilleur de ne le faire poinct, et d'avoir la paix, pour l'avoir plus [?honnorable]
[l. 24] et [?advantageuse], c'est quand il fault [47] […] [l. 25] faire congnoistre que l'on […], et qu'il
seroit nécessaire de [l. 26] faire de plus grands effects.

Ie recours à vous comme à tous [?en] [l. 27] particulier ami, pour me donner en cela vostre advis, ainsi que ie
sçai que [l. 28] vous le pouvés, remectant mesmes à vostre discretion de faire sç[l. 29]avoir au roi la presense
le roi de Navarre où i'en suis, car si l'on veult ainsi dissiper [l. 30] ceste armée, et qu'elle demeure sans
secours, il est inévitable qu'[l. 31]elle périra toute entière avant qu'il soit [?six] sepmaines, ce que ie [l. 32]
vouldrois éviter pour le moins tant qu'elle sera entre mes mains.

Sa [l. 33] Maiesté en perdra plus que moi.

Que toutesfois ie merite [?poinct] de rece[l. 34]voir cest affront, car il se peult asseurer qu'il ne sera iamais
plus [l. 35] fidellemant [?servi] de personne que de moi, que ie suis tant confirmé en [l. 36] ceste resolution
que ie prie à Dieu qu'il me ruine plustost que […] [l. 37] iamais.

Et qu'il vous donne, Monsieur de Villeroy, tout le contan[l. 38]tement que vous désirés.

De Villeneufve d'Agenoys, ce […] jour de Mars 1586 [clear].

# f. 110, Mayenne to Villeroy, Villeneuve-d'Agenais, March 1586: English translation

Translation of `reading_f110.md` (pass 3, 22 Oct 2026), with the same holes. Square brackets with a question mark: a
word carried only in part by its figures; […]: figures that do not form a word; [45], [47]: code groups (47 =
all/every). Words in round brackets are added in English for sense and have no figures behind them. Line numbers are
those of the manuscript.

[l. 1] [?Sir], [?Villeroy],

you will see well by the letter that I am writing to the King […] [l. 2] […] [?held back] from […] the most […]
that I […] [l. 3] [?reserved] about [?speaking] more freely with you, with what (he) had always made [l. 4]
[?much] of the great [?confidence] and particular friendship, (which) I [?recognised] [l. 5] from the beginning.

For the little […] that I have seen this [l. 6] army have, that it was of little [?consideration], and that one
did not expect [l. 7] much good effect from it, […] more particularly remarked in that [l. 8] passage which is in
your last despatch, that the King has taken […] [l. 9] the dangers that befall those who undertake war without
money.

[l. 10] As he has done […] [45]-ment, that [47] […] words, he has had […] [l. 11] no more substance.

Restraint appears from it. One must speak plainly.

[l. 12] If the King is tired of the war, and wishes to end it, it can be done [l. 13] more [?honourably] than by
the ruin of my reputation, and than by exposing me [l. 14] to press for the war […] [?to go on], […] that one […]
could sustain it [l. 15] […] the King of Navarre [45] [?his] enemies who want it, then help this means.

For [l. 16] their argument may fail.

For the rest, I can say that […] this army [l. 17] has not had better [?results], that it is not its fault; that on
the contrary [l. 18] nothing better could be done than to preserve it, as I have done, [l. 19] at little expense, in
order to use it at the right season and on the occasions which [l. 20] [?present themselves] now.

As for the war, it can be made, and usefully, [l. 21] if [?only] the promise is kept to send the money which was
earmarked [l. 22] for it, to which the obstruction of the clergy is not contrary.

But if [l. 23] His Majesty judges it better not to make it, and to have peace, so as to have it more
[?honourable] [l. 24] and [?advantageous], it is when one must [47] […] [l. 25] make it known that one […],
and that it would be necessary to [l. 26] make greater efforts.

I turn to you, as to all [?in] [l. 27] particular friend (*comme à tous [?en] particulier ami*), to give me your advice on this, as I know [l. 28] you can,
leaving even to your discretion the making known [l. 29] to the King (of) the presence (of) the King of Navarre where
I am, for if they mean thus to scatter [l. 30] this army, and it remains without help, it is inevitable that
[l. 31] it will perish entirely before [?six] weeks are out, which I [l. 32] would wish to avoid at least so long as
it is in my hands.

His [l. 33] Majesty will lose more by it than I.

That nevertheless I [?do] [?not] deserve to receive [l. 34] this affront (the figures give *ie merite poinct*,
without *ne*), for he may be assured that he will never be [l. 35] more faithfully [?served] by anyone than by me,
who am so confirmed in [l. 36] this resolution that I pray God he ruin me sooner than […] [l. 37] ever.

And may He give you, Monsieur de Villeroy, all the contentment [l. 38] you desire.

From Villeneufve d'Agenoys, the […] day of March 1586 [clear].

fr. 15571 f. 177r-v read in part

Forget, 31 December 1585, on Mayenne's design for Gascony: a plan on Pons and Taillebourg, with Taillebourg's toll on the Charente and thirty thousand ecus to be raised from the country, given up when the vicomte de Turenne took the field with guns. The verso (11 lines) continues it.

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (95% here); the holes are marked in the reading.
